\displaystyle\exp\left(-c_{1}M_{n}\log n\right)+\exp\left(-c_{2}M_{n}\log n\right).

(S.4)

Therefore, by applying the Markov’s inequality and the Borel-Cantelli Lemma to (
S1
), the numerator of
T_{1}
in (
S1
) is smaller than
\exp\left\{-\min(c_{1},c_{2})M_{n}\log n/2\right\}
almost surely
\mathbb{P}_{(\theta_{0},\alpha_{0},\tau_{0},\beta_{0})}
as
n\to\infty
. On the other hand, Theorem
1
shows that almost surely
\mathbb{P}_{(\theta_{0},\alpha_{0},\tau_{0},\beta_{0})}
as
n\to\infty
, for all
d\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
, the denominator in
T_{1}
in (
S1
) is lower bounded by

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{p}}\exp\left\{\Lcal_{n}(\theta^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime},\tau^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})-\Lcal_{n}(\theta_{0},\alpha_{0},\tau_{0},\beta_{0})\right\}

\displaystyle~~\times\pi(\theta^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime},\tau^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})\mathrm{d}\theta^{\prime}\mathrm{d}\tau^{\prime}\mathrm{d}\alpha^{\prime}\mathrm{d}\beta^{\prime}\geq Dn^{-(3p+2+2/\kappa)},

(S.5)

for some constant
D>0
. Hence for all sufficiently large
n
, the first term
T_{1}
in (
S1
) is upper bounded by

\displaystyle T_{1}

\displaystyle\leq D^{-1}n^{(3p+2+2/\kappa)}\exp\left\{-\min(c_{1},c_{2})M_{n}\log n/2\right\}\leq\exp(-c^{\prime}\log n)\to 0,

(S.6)

for an arbitrary
c^{\prime}>1
, almost surely
\mathbb{P}_{(\theta_{0},\alpha_{0},\tau_{0},\beta_{0})}
.

For the second term
T_{2}
in (
S1
), by Assumption
4
and the Fubini’s theorem, the numerator has expectation

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{(\theta_{0},\alpha_{0},\tau_{0},\beta_{0})}(1-\phi_{n})\int_{\Bcal_{0}(\varepsilon_{1n},\varepsilon_{2n})^{c}\cap\Ecal_{n}^{c}}\exp\Big\{\Lcal_{n}(\theta,\alpha,\tau,\beta)

\displaystyle\quad-\Lcal_{n}(\theta_{0},\alpha_{0},\tau_{0},\beta_{0})\Big\}\pi(\theta,\alpha,\tau,\beta)\mathrm{d}\theta\mathrm{d}\tau\mathrm{d}\alpha\mathrm{d}\beta

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\int_{\Bcal_{0}(\varepsilon_{1n},\varepsilon_{2n})^{c}\cap\Ecal_{n}^{c}}{\mathbb{E}}_{(\theta_{0},\alpha_{0},\tau_{0},\beta_{0})}(1-\phi_{n})\exp\Big\{\Lcal_{n}(\theta,\alpha,\tau,\beta)

\displaystyle\quad-\Lcal_{n}(\theta_{0},\alpha_{0},\tau_{0},\beta_{0})\Big\}\pi(\theta,\alpha,\tau,\beta)\mathrm{d}\theta\mathrm{d}\tau\mathrm{d}\alpha\mathrm{d}\beta

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\int_{\Bcal_{0}(\varepsilon_{1n},\varepsilon_{2n})^{c}\cap\Ecal_{n}^{c}}{\mathbb{E}}_{(\theta,\alpha,\tau,\beta)}(1-\phi_{n})\pi(\theta,\alpha,\tau,\beta)\mathrm{d}\theta\mathrm{d}\tau\mathrm{d}\alpha\mathrm{d}\beta

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle\int_{\Ecal_{n}^{c}}1\cdot\pi(\theta,\alpha,\tau,\beta)\mathrm{d}\theta\mathrm{d}\tau\mathrm{d}\alpha\mathrm{d}\beta=\Pi(\Ecal_{n}^{c})\leq n^{-(3p+4+2/\kappa)}.

(S.7)

Therefore, by applying the Markov’s inequality to (
S1
), the numerator of
T_{2}
in (
S1
) is smaller than
n^{-\left(3p+\frac{5}{2}+\frac{2}{\kappa}\right)}
with probability at least
1-n^{-3/2}
. Since the sequence
\{n^{-3/2}:n\geq 1\}
is summable over
n
, by the Borel-Cantelli Lemma, we have that the numerator of
T_{2}
in (
S1
) is smaller than
n^{-\left(3p+\frac{5}{2}+\frac{2}{\kappa}\right)}
almost surely
\mathbb{P}_{(\theta_{0},\alpha_{0},\tau_{0},\beta_{0})}
as
n\to\infty
.
We combine this with the lower bound in (
S1
) to conclude that almost surely
\mathbb{P}_{(\theta_{0},\alpha_{0},\tau_{0},\beta_{0})}
as
n\to\infty
, the second term
T_{2}
in (
S1
) is upper bounded by

\displaystyle T_{2}

\displaystyle\leq D^{-1}n^{3p+2+2/\kappa}\cdot n^{-\left(3p+\frac{5}{2}+\frac{2}{\kappa}\right)}=D^{-1}n^{-1/2}\to 0.

(S.8)

Finally, we combine (
S1
), (
S1
), (
S.6
), and (
S.8
) to conclude that the right-hand side of (
S1
) converges to zero almost surely
\mathbb{P}_{(\theta_{0},\alpha_{0},\tau_{0},\beta_{0})}
as
n\to\infty
, which completes the proof of Theorem
2
.
∎

S2
Proof of Theorems
1
and Auxiliary Technical Lemmas

S2.1
Proof of Theorem
1

Proof.

Define the sets
\Acal_{n}
and
\Dcal_{n}
as

\displaystyle\Acal_{n}=\left\{(\theta,\alpha,\tau,\beta)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{p}:~|\alpha/\alpha_{0}-1|\leq n^{-2/\kappa}\right\},

\displaystyle\Dcal_{n}=\left\{(\theta,\alpha,\tau,\beta)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{p}:~\|\beta-\beta_{0}\|\leq n^{-3}\right\}.

By the continuity of the prior density in Assumption
3
, we have that for all sufficiently large
n
,
\pi(\theta,\tau,\alpha,\beta)>\pi(\theta_{0},\tau_{0},\alpha_{0},\beta_{0})/2>0
for all
(\theta,\tau,\alpha,\beta)\in\Bcal_{0}(n^{-1},n^{-1})\cap\Acal_{n}\cap\Dcal_{n}
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\Pi\left(\Bcal_{0}(n^{-1},n^{-1})\cap\Acal_{n}\cap\Dcal_{n}\right)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\pi(\theta_{0},\tau_{0},\alpha_{0},\beta_{0})}{2}\cdot(2\theta_{0}n^{-1})\cdot(2\tau_{0}n^{-1})\cdot(2\alpha_{0}n^{-2/\kappa})\cdot v_{0}n^{-3p}

\displaystyle=4v_{0}\theta_{0}\tau_{0}\alpha_{0}\pi(\theta_{0},\tau_{0},\alpha_{0},\beta_{0})n^{-(3p+2+2/\kappa)},

(S.9)

where
v_{0}=\pi^{p/2}/\Gamma(p/2+1)
. Using the definition of log-likelihood in (
2
), the exponent in Theorem
1
can be written as

\displaystyle\Lcal_{n}(\theta,\alpha,\tau,\beta)-\Lcal_{n}(\theta_{0},\alpha_{0},\tau_{0},\beta_{0})

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\left\{\tr\left[\left\{\theta K_{\alpha,\nu}(S_{n})+\tau I_{n}\right\}^{-1}\widetilde{Y}_{n}\widetilde{Y}_{n}^{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}\right]-\tr\left[\left\{\theta_{0}K_{\alpha_{0},\nu}(S_{n})+\tau_{0}I_{n}\right\}^{-1}\widetilde{Y}_{n}\widetilde{Y}_{n}^{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}\right]\right\}

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\log\frac{\dett\left\{\theta K_{\alpha,\nu}(S_{n})+\tau I_{n}\right\}}{\dett\left\{\theta_{0}K_{\alpha_{0},\nu}(S_{n})+\tau_{0}I_{n}\right\}}

\displaystyle+\widetilde{Y}_{n}^{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}\left[\left\{\theta K_{\alpha,\nu}(S_{n})+\tau I_{n}\right\}^{-1}-\left\{\theta_{0}K_{\alpha_{0},\nu}(S_{n})+\tau_{0}I_{n}\right\}^{-1}\right]F_{n}(\beta-\beta_{0})

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}(\beta-\beta_{0})^{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}F_{n}^{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}\left[\left\{\theta K_{\alpha,\nu}(S_{n})+\tau I_{n}\right\}^{-1}-\left\{\theta_{0}K_{\alpha_{0},\nu}(S_{n})+\tau_{0}I_{n}\right\}^{-1}\right]F_{n}(\beta-\beta_{0}).

Using the lower bounds in Part (i) of Lemma
S.5
and Lemma
S.6
in Section
S2.2
below, we obtain that there exists a large integer
N_{1}
that depends on
\nu,d,C_{\mathrm{f}},\alpha_{0},\theta_{0}
,
\tau_{0},L,r_{0},\kappa
, such that for any
\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}\in(0,1/2)
, for all
n>N_{1}
, with probability at least
1-3\exp(-\log^{2}n)
,

\displaystyle\int_{\Bcal_{0}(\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2})\cap\Acal_{n}\cap\Dcal_{n}}\exp\left\{\Lcal_{n}(\theta,\alpha,\tau,\beta)-\Lcal_{n}(\theta_{0},\alpha_{0},\tau_{0},\beta_{0})\right\}\cdot\pi(\theta,\alpha,\tau,\beta)~\mathrm{d}\theta\mathrm{d}\tau\mathrm{d}\alpha\mathrm{d}\beta

\displaystyle\geq\Pi(\Bcal_{0}(\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2})\cap\Acal_{n}\cap\Dcal_{n})\cdot\exp\Big\{-\left(\frac{5}{2}n\epsilon_{1\vee 2}+5Ln^{-1}\right)

\displaystyle\quad-\left[8C_{\mathrm{f}}p^{1/2}\left\{\theta_{0}K_{\alpha_{0},\nu}(0)+\tau_{0}\right\}^{1/2}\tau_{0}^{-1}n^{-1}+2C_{\mathrm{f}}^{2}p\tau_{0}^{-1}n^{-4}\right]\Big\},

(S.10)

where
\epsilon_{1\vee 2}=\max(\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2})
. We can take
\epsilon_{1}=\epsilon_{2}=n^{-1}
in (
S2.1
), so
\epsilon_{1\vee 2}=n^{-1}
. We then combine (
S2.1
) and (
S2.1
) to obtain that for all sufficiently large
n>N_{1}
, with probability at least
1-3\exp(-\log^{2}n)
,

\displaystyle\int\exp\left\{\Lcal_{n}(\theta,\alpha,\tau,\beta)-\Lcal_{n}(\theta_{0},\alpha_{0},\tau_{0},\beta_{0})\right\}\cdot\pi(\theta,\alpha,\tau,\beta)~\mathrm{d}\theta\mathrm{d}\tau\mathrm{d}\alpha\mathrm{d}\beta

\displaystyle\geq{}

\displaystyle\int_{\Bcal_{0}(n^{-1},n^{-1})\cap\Acal_{n}\cap\Dcal_{n}}\exp\left\{\Lcal_{n}(\theta,\alpha,\tau,\beta)-\Lcal_{n}(\theta_{0},\alpha_{0},\tau_{0},\beta_{0})\right\}\cdot\pi(\theta,\alpha,\tau,\beta)~\mathrm{d}\theta\mathrm{d}\tau\mathrm{d}\alpha\mathrm{d}\beta

\displaystyle\geq{}

\displaystyle\Pi\left(\Bcal_{0}\big(n^{-1},n^{-1}\big)\cap\Acal_{n}\cap\Dcal_{n}\right)\cdot\exp\Big\{-\Big(\frac{5}{2}n\cdot n^{-1}+5Ln^{-1}\Big)

\displaystyle\quad-\left[8C_{\mathrm{f}}p^{1/2}\left\{\theta_{0}K_{\alpha_{0},\nu}(0)+\tau_{0}\right\}^{1/2}\tau_{0}^{-1}n^{-1}+2C_{\mathrm{f}}^{2}p\tau_{0}^{-1}n^{-4}\right]\Big\}

\displaystyle\geq{}

\displaystyle\Pi\left(\Bcal_{0}\big(n^{-1},n^{-1}\big)\cap\Acal_{n}\cap\Dcal_{n}\right)\exp\left[-2.5-5L-8C_{\mathrm{f}}p^{1/2}\left\{\theta_{0}K_{\alpha_{0},\nu}(0)+\tau_{0}\right\}^{1/2}\tau_{0}^{-1}-2C_{\mathrm{f}}^{2}p\tau_{0}^{-1}\right]

\displaystyle\geq{}

\displaystyle Dn^{-(3p+2+2/\kappa)},

(S.11)

where

D=4v_{0}\theta_{0}\tau_{0}\alpha_{0}\pi(\theta_{0},\tau_{0},\alpha_{0},\beta_{0})\cdot\exp\left[-2.5-5L-8C_{\mathrm{f}}p^{1/2}\left\{\theta_{0}K_{\alpha_{0},\nu}(0)+\tau_{0}\right\}^{1/2}\tau_{0}^{-1}-2C_{\mathrm{f}}^{2}p\tau_{0}^{-1}\right]

is a positive constant. This completes the proof of Theorem
1
.
∎

S2.2
Spectral Analysis of Covariance Functions

We present a series of results for the spectral analysis of the covariance function
\theta K_{\alpha,\nu}
for the Gaussian process
X(\cdot)
, which is used in the proof of Theorem
1
.

For
w\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, let

\displaystyle f_{\theta,\alpha,\nu}(w)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\exp\left(-\imath w^{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}x\right)\theta K_{\alpha,\nu}(x)\mathrm{d}x

(S.12)

be the spectral density of the covariance function
\theta K_{\alpha,\nu}
. For any given pair
(\theta,\alpha)
, let
\|h\|_{f_{\theta,\alpha,\nu}}^{2}=\langle h,h\rangle_{f_{\theta,\alpha,\nu}}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|h(w)|^{2}f_{\theta,\alpha,\nu}(w)\mathrm{d}w
be the norm of a generic function
h
in the Hilbert space
L_{2}(f_{\theta,\alpha,\nu})
, with inner product
\langle h_{1},h_{2}\rangle_{f_{\theta,\alpha,\nu}}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}h_{1}(w)\overline{h_{2}(w)}f_{\theta,\alpha,\nu}(w)\mathrm{d}w
for any functions
h_{1},h_{2}\in L_{2}(f_{\theta,\alpha,\nu})
.

The following lemma is important for our spectral analysis.

Lemma S.1
.

Let
K_{\alpha,\nu}(S_{n})
be the
n\times n
covariance matrix whose
(i,j)
-entry is
K_{\alpha,\nu}(\mathbf{s}_{i}-\mathbf{s}_{j})
. Then for any
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
, there exists an
n\times n
invertible matrix
U_{\alpha}
that depends on
\alpha,\alpha_{0},\theta_{0},\nu,S_{n}
, such that

\displaystyle\theta_{0}U_{\alpha}^{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}K_{\alpha_{0},\nu}(S_{n})U_{\alpha}=I_{n},\qquad\theta_{0}U_{\alpha}^{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}K_{\alpha,\nu}(S_{n})U_{\alpha}=\Lambda_{n}(\alpha)=\diag\{\lambda_{k,n}(\alpha):k=1,\ldots,n\},

(S.13)

where
\{\lambda_{k,n}(\alpha),k=1,\ldots,n\}
are the positive diagonal entries of the diagonal matrix
\Lambda_{n}(\alpha)
.

Furthermore, there exist orthonormal basis functions
\psi_{1},\ldots,\psi_{n}\in L_{2}(f_{\theta_{0},\alpha_{0},\nu})
, such that for any
j,k\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
,

\displaystyle\langle\psi_{j},\psi_{k}\rangle_{f_{\theta_{0},\alpha_{0},\nu}}=\Ical(j=k),\qquad\langle\psi_{j},\psi_{k}\rangle_{f_{\theta,\alpha,\nu}}=\lambda_{j,n}(\alpha)\Ical(j=k),

(S.14)

where
\Ical(\cdot)
is the indicator function.

Proof of
Lemma
S.1
.

The existence of such an invertible
U_{\alpha}
is guaranteed by Theorem 7.6.4 and Corollary 7.6.5 on page 465–466 of
Horn and
Johnson 1985
. For completeness, we directly prove the existence of such an invertible matrix in the following general claim.

Claim:
Suppose that
A
and
B
are two generic
n\times n
symmetric positive definite matrices. Then there always exists an invertible matrix
U
, such that

\displaystyle U^{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}AU=I_{n},\qquad U^{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}BU=\Lambda,

(S.15)

where
I_{n}
is the
n\times n
identity matrix and
\Lambda
is an
n\times n
diagonal matrix whose diagonal entries are all positive.

Proof of the Claim:

Since
B
is symmetric positive definite, let
B=LL^{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}
be the Cholesky decomposition of
B
, where
L
is an
n\times n
lower triangular matrix with all positive diagonal entries and
L
is invertible. Let
G=L^{-1}AL^{-{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}}
. Then obviously
G
is also a symmetric positive definite matrix with
G^{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}=G
. Suppose that
G
has the spectral decomposition
G=PDP^{-1}
where
P
is an
n\times n
orthogonal matrix (
P^{-1}=P^{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}
) and
D
is a
n\times n
diagonal matrix whose diagonal entries are all eigenvalues of
G
and they are all positive. Then
P^{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}GP=D
. We let
U=L^{-{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}}PD^{-1/2}
. It follows that

\displaystyle U^{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}AU

\displaystyle=D^{-1/2}P^{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}L^{-1}AL^{-{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}}PD^{-1/2}

\displaystyle=D^{-1/2}P^{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}GPD^{-1/2}=D^{-1/2}DD^{-1/2}=I_{n},

\displaystyle U^{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}BU

\displaystyle=D^{-1/2}P^{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}L^{-1}BL^{-{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}}PD^{-1/2}

\displaystyle=D^{-1/2}P^{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}L^{-1}LL^{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}L^{-{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}}PD^{-1/2}=D^{-1/2}P^{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}PD^{-1/2}=D^{-1}.

We set
\Lambda=D^{-1}
which is an
n\times n
diagonal matrix whose diagonal entries are all positive. This proves the claim.

Based on the claim, if we set
A=\theta_{0}K_{\alpha_{0},\nu}(S_{n})
and
B=\theta_{0}K_{\alpha,\nu}(S_{n})
, then we can find an invertible matrix
U
such that (
S.15
) holds. Because
\theta_{0},\alpha_{0},\nu
are assumed to be fixed numbers, we can see that
U
only changes with
\alpha
and therefore we can write it as
U_{\alpha}
. Similarly, we can write
\Lambda_{n}(\alpha)
to highlight its dependence on
\alpha
and
n
. Correspondingly, we have
\theta_{0}U_{\alpha}^{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}K_{\alpha_{0},\nu}(S_{n})U_{\alpha}=I_{n}
and
\theta_{0}U_{\alpha}^{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}K_{\alpha,\nu}(S_{n})U_{\alpha}=\diag\{\lambda_{k,n}(\alpha):k=1,\ldots,n\}=\Lambda_{n}(\alpha)
. This proves (
S.13
). The existence of orthonormal basis functions
\psi_{1},\ldots,\psi_{n}\in L_{2}(f_{\theta_{0},\alpha_{0},\nu})
is proved in Section 4 of
Wang and Loh 2011
, which does not involve the specific forms of covariance functions. This completes the proof of Lemma
S.1
.
∎

Lemma S.2
.

Suppose that Assumption
2
holds. Then for
\{\lambda_{k,n}(\alpha):k=1,\ldots,n\}
defined in Lemma
S.1
and any
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
,

\displaystyle\sup_{|\alpha/\alpha_{0}-1|\leq r_{0}}\lambda_{k,n}(\alpha)\leq 1+L|\alpha/\alpha_{0}-1|^{\kappa},

(S.16)

\displaystyle\inf_{|\alpha/\alpha_{0}-1|\leq r_{0}}\lambda_{k,n}(\alpha)\geq 1-L|\alpha/\alpha_{0}-1|^{\kappa},

(S.17)

for all
k=1,\ldots,n
. Furthermore, if
\alpha\leq\alpha_{0}
, then
\lambda_{k,n}(\alpha)\geq 1
for all
k=1,\ldots,n
; if
\alpha\geq\alpha_{0}
, then
0\leq\lambda_{k,n}(\alpha)\leq 1
for all
k=1,\ldots,n
.

Proof of
Lemma
S.2
.

For (
S.16
) and (
S.17
), we use the relation

\lambda_{k,n}(\alpha)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|\psi_{k}(w)|^{2}f_{\theta_{0},\alpha_{0},\nu}(w)\cdot\frac{f_{\theta_{0},\alpha,\nu}(w)}{f_{\theta_{0},\alpha_{0},\nu}(w)}\mathrm{d}w

for
k=1,\ldots,n
together with the bounds in Assumption
2
(i) to obtain that for all
\alpha
satisfying
|\alpha/\alpha_{0}-1|\leq r_{0}
,

\displaystyle\lambda_{k,n}(\alpha)

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{w\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{f_{\theta_{0},\alpha,\nu}(w)}{f_{\theta_{0},\alpha_{0},\nu}(w)}\cdot\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|\psi_{k}(w)|^{2}f_{\theta_{0},\alpha_{0},\nu}(w)\mathrm{d}w\leq 1+L|\alpha/\alpha_{0}-1|^{\kappa},

\displaystyle\lambda_{k,n}(\alpha)

\displaystyle\geq\inf_{w\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{f_{\theta_{0},\alpha,\nu}(w)}{f_{\theta_{0},\alpha_{0},\nu}(w)}\cdot\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|\psi_{k}(w)|^{2}f_{\theta_{0},\alpha_{0},\nu}(w)\mathrm{d}w\geq 1-L|\alpha/\alpha_{0}-1|^{\kappa}.

(S.18)

This proves (
S.16
) and (
S.17
).

By the non-increasing property of
f_{\theta,\alpha,\nu}(w)
in
\alpha
in Assumption
2
(ii), we have that if
\alpha\leq\alpha_{0}
,
f_{\theta_{0},\alpha,\nu}(w)\geq f_{\theta_{0},\alpha_{0},\nu}(w)
for all
w\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and for all
k=1,\ldots,n
,

\displaystyle\lambda_{k,n}(\alpha)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|\psi_{k}(w)|^{2}f_{\theta_{0},\alpha,\nu}(w)\mathrm{d}w\geq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|\psi_{k}(w)|^{2}f_{\theta_{0},\alpha_{0},\nu}(w)\mathrm{d}w=1.

The conclusion for
\alpha\geq\alpha_{0}
follows similarly.
∎

Lemma S.3
.

Suppose that Assumption
2
(ii) holds. For any
\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that
\alpha\leq\alpha^{\prime}
, we have that
K_{\alpha,\nu}(S_{n})\succeq K_{\alpha^{\prime},\nu}(S_{n})
, for any set
S_{n}=\{s_{1},\ldots,s_{n}\}
of
n
distinct locations in
[0,1]^{d}
.

Proof of
Lemma
S.3
.

The proof of Lemma
S.3
is motivated by the proof of Lemma 1 in
Kaufman and
Shaby 2013
. We define the matrix
\Omega^{\dagger}=K_{\alpha,\nu}(S_{n})-K_{\alpha^{\prime},\nu}(S_{n})
. Then the entries of
\Omega^{\dagger}
can be expressed in terms of a function
\widetilde{K}_{\Omega^{\dagger}}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
, with its
(i,j)
-entry

\displaystyle\Omega^{\dagger}_{ij}

\displaystyle=\widetilde{K}_{\Omega^{\dagger}}(\mathbf{s}_{i}-\mathbf{s}_{j})=K_{\alpha,\nu}(\mathbf{s}_{i}-\mathbf{s}_{j})-K_{\alpha^{\prime},\nu}(\mathbf{s}_{i}-\mathbf{s}_{j}),

for
i,j\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
. The matrix
\Omega^{\dagger}
is positive definite if
\widetilde{K}_{\Omega^{\dagger}}
is a positive semidefinite function.

By the non-increasing property of
f_{\theta,\alpha,\nu}(w)
in
\alpha
for any given
\theta>0
and
w\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
as in Assumption
2
(ii), we have that for
\alpha\leq\alpha^{\prime}
,

\displaystyle f_{\theta,\alpha,\nu}(w)

\displaystyle\geq f_{\theta,\alpha^{\prime},\nu}(w).

(S.19)

Therefore, we can compute the spectral density of the function
\widetilde{K}_{\Omega^{\dagger}}
:

\displaystyle f_{\Omega^{\dagger}}(w)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\exp\left(-\imath w^{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}x\right)\widetilde{K}_{\Omega^{\dagger}}(x)\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=f_{1,\alpha,\nu}(w)-f_{1,\alpha^{\prime},\nu}(w)\geq 0,\text{ for all }w\in\mathbb{R}^{d},

(S.20)

where the last step follows from (
S.19
). This has shown that
\widetilde{K}_{\Omega^{\dagger}}
is indeed a positive semidefinite function. Therefore,
\Omega^{\dagger}
is a positive semidefinite matrix and the conclusion follows.
∎

Lemma S.4
.

Suppose that Assumption
2
holds. Let
\Acal_{n}^{\dagger}=\left\{\alpha\in\mathbb{R}_{+}:|\alpha/\alpha_{0}-1|\leq n^{-2/\kappa}\right\}
where
\kappa
is as defined in Assumption
2
. There exists a large integer
N_{2}
that only depends on
\nu,d,\alpha_{0},L,r_{0},\kappa
, such that for all
n>N_{2}
,

\displaystyle\sup_{\alpha\in\Acal_{n}^{\dagger}}\left\{\sum_{k=1}^{n}\left|\lambda_{k,n}(\alpha)-1\right|\right\}\leq Ln^{-1},\qquad\sup_{\alpha\in\Acal_{n}^{\dagger}}\left\{\sum_{k=1}^{n}\left|\lambda_{k,n}(\alpha)-1\right|^{2}\right\}\leq L^{2}n^{-3},

\displaystyle\sup_{\alpha\in\Acal_{n}^{\dagger}}\left\{\sum_{k=1}^{n}\left|\lambda_{k,n}(\alpha)^{-1}-1\right|\right\}\leq 2Ln^{-1},\qquad\sup_{\alpha\in\Acal_{n}^{\dagger}}\left\{\sum_{k=1}^{n}\left|\lambda_{k,n}(\alpha)^{-1}-1\right|^{2}\right\}\leq 4L^{2}n^{-3}.

(S.21)

Proof of
Lemma
S.4
.

For all sufficiently large
n
,
n^{-2/\kappa}<r_{0}
for
r_{0}
in Assumption
2
. By Lemma
S.2
, we have that for any
k=1,\ldots,n
, for all
\alpha\in\Acal_{n}^{\dagger}
,

\displaystyle\quad~\left|\lambda_{k,n}(\alpha)-1\right|\leq L|\alpha/\alpha_{0}-1|^{\kappa}.

Therefore, uniformly over all
\alpha\in\Acal_{n}^{\dagger}
,

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{n}\left|\lambda_{k,n}(\alpha)-1\right|\leq n\cdot L|\alpha/\alpha_{0}-1|^{\kappa}\leq Ln^{-1},

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{n}\left|\lambda_{k,n}(\alpha)-1\right|^{2}\leq n\cdot L^{2}|\alpha/\alpha_{0}-1|^{2\kappa}\leq L^{2}n^{-3},

(S.22)

which has proved the first two inequalities in (
S.4
).

For all
\alpha\in\Acal_{n}^{\dagger}
, for all sufficiently large
n
,

1-L|\alpha/\alpha_{0}-1|^{\kappa}\geq 1-Ln^{-2}\geq\frac{1}{2}.

Therefore, using (
S.17
) in Lemma
S.2
and (
S2.2
), we have that uniformly for all
\alpha\in\Acal_{n}^{\dagger}
, for all sufficiently large
n
,

\displaystyle\min_{1\leq k\leq n}\lambda_{k,n}(\alpha)\geq 1/2,\quad\max_{1\leq k\leq n}\lambda_{k,n}(\alpha)^{-1}\leq 2,

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{n}\left|\lambda_{k,n}(\alpha)^{-1}-1\right|=\frac{\sum_{k=1}^{n}\left|\lambda_{k,n}(\alpha)-1\right|}{\min_{1\leq k\leq n}\lambda_{k,n}(\alpha)}\leq 2Ln^{-1},

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{n}\left|\lambda_{k,n}(\alpha)^{-1}-1\right|^{2}=\frac{\sum_{k=1}^{n}\left|\lambda_{k,n}(\alpha)-1\right|}{\min_{1\leq k\leq n}\lambda_{k,n}(\alpha)^{2}}\leq 4L^{2}n^{-3}.

which proves the last two inequalities in (
S.4
).
∎

S2.3
Technical Lemmas for Evidence Lower Bound

Let
\widetilde{Y}_{n}=Y_{n}-F_{n}\beta_{0}
. Then
\widetilde{Y}_{n}\sim\Ncal(0,\theta_{0}K_{\alpha_{0},\nu}(S_{n})+\tau_{0}I_{n})
, and
Y_{n}-F_{n}\beta=\widetilde{Y}_{n}-F_{n}(\beta-\beta_{0})
.

Lemma S.5
.

Suppose that Assumptions
1
and
2
hold. Let
\Acal_{n}=\big\{(\theta,\alpha,\tau,\beta)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{p}:

|\alpha/\alpha_{0}-1|\leq n^{-2/\kappa}\big\}
. There exists a large integer
N_{3}
that only depends on
\nu,d,\theta_{0},\alpha_{0},L,r_{0},\kappa
, such that for any
\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}\in(0,1/2)
, for all
n>N_{3}
, with probability at least
1-2\exp(-\log^{2}n)
,

\displaystyle\inf_{\Bcal_{0}(\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2})\cap\Acal_{n}}\Bigg\{-\frac{1}{2}\left(\tr\left[\left\{\theta K_{\alpha,\nu}(S_{n})+\tau I_{n}\right\}^{-1}\widetilde{Y}_{n}\widetilde{Y}_{n}^{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}\right]-\tr\left[\left\{\theta_{0}K_{\alpha_{0},\nu}(S_{n})+\tau_{0}I_{n}\right\}^{-1}\widetilde{Y}_{n}\widetilde{Y}_{n}^{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}\right]\right)

\displaystyle\qquad-\frac{1}{2}\log\frac{\dett\left\{\theta K_{\alpha,\nu}(S_{n})+\tau I_{n}\right\}}{\dett\left\{\theta_{0}K_{\alpha_{0},\nu}(S_{n})+\tau_{0}I_{n}\right\}}\Bigg\}

\displaystyle\geq-\left(\frac{5}{2}n\epsilon_{1\vee 2}+5Ln^{-1}\right),

(S.23)

where
\epsilon_{1\vee 2}=\max(\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2})
.

Proof of
Lemma
S.5
.

There are two terms inside the infimum in (
S.5
). We provide lower bounds for each of them. For notational simplicity, we define

\displaystyle\Omega_{\alpha}=\theta_{0}K_{\alpha,\nu}(S_{n})+\tau_{0}I_{n},\quad\Omega_{0}=\theta_{0}K_{\alpha_{0},\nu}(S_{n})+\tau_{0}I_{n}.

(S.24)

Lower bound for the first term in (
S.5
):

In the set
\Bcal_{0}(\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2})
,
\theta>(1-\epsilon_{1\vee 2})\theta_{0}
and
\tau>(1-\epsilon_{1\vee 2})\tau_{0}
. Since
K_{\alpha,\nu}(S_{n})
is positive definite, we have that
\theta K_{\alpha,\nu}(S_{n})+\tau I_{n}\succeq(1-\epsilon_{1\vee 2})\left\{\theta_{0}K_{\alpha,\nu}(S_{n})+\tau_{0}I_{n}\right\}
, and hence
\left\{\theta K_{\alpha,\nu}(S_{n})+\tau I_{n}\right\}^{-1}\preceq(1-\epsilon_{1\vee 2})^{-1}\left\{\theta_{0}K_{\alpha,\nu}(S_{n})+\tau_{0}I_{n}\right\}^{-1}
. Therefore, for
\widetilde{Y}_{n}=Y_{n}-F_{n}\beta_{0}\sim\Ncal(0,\theta_{0}K_{\alpha_{0},\nu}(S_{n})+\tau_{0}I_{n})
, by Lemma
S.7
Part (ii), we have that

\displaystyle\tr\left[\left\{\theta K_{\alpha,\nu}(S_{n})+\tau I_{n}\right\}^{-1}\widetilde{Y}_{n}\widetilde{Y}_{n}^{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}\right]-\tr\left[\left\{\theta_{0}K_{\alpha_{0},\nu}(S_{n})+\tau_{0}I_{n}\right\}^{-1}\widetilde{Y}_{n}\widetilde{Y}_{n}^{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle(1-\epsilon_{1\vee 2})^{-1}\tr\left[\left\{\theta_{0}K_{\alpha,\nu}(S_{n})+\tau_{0}I_{n}\right\}^{-1}\widetilde{Y}_{n}\widetilde{Y}_{n}^{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}\right]-\tr\left[\left\{\theta_{0}K_{\alpha_{0},\nu}(S_{n})+\tau_{0}I_{n}\right\}^{-1}\widetilde{Y}_{n}\widetilde{Y}_{n}^{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}\right]

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle(1-\epsilon_{1\vee 2})^{-1}\left\{\tr\left(\Omega_{\alpha}^{-1}\widetilde{Y}_{n}\widetilde{Y}_{n}^{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}\right)-\tr\left(\Omega_{0}^{-1}\widetilde{Y}_{n}\widetilde{Y}_{n}^{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}\right)\right\}+\frac{\epsilon_{1\vee 2}}{1-\epsilon_{1\vee 2}}\tr\left(\Omega_{0}^{-1}\widetilde{Y}_{n}\widetilde{Y}_{n}^{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}\right).

(S.25)

We derive an upper bound for the first term inside the bracket in (
S2.3
). Using (
S.13
) in Lemma
S.1
, we have that
\theta_{0}K_{\alpha,\nu}(S_{n})=U_{\alpha}^{-{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}}\Lambda_{n}(\alpha)U_{\alpha}^{-1}
and
\theta_{0}K_{\alpha_{0},\nu}(S_{n})=U_{\alpha}^{-{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}}U_{\alpha}^{-1}
. Therefore, we repeatedly apply the Sherman-Morrison-Woodbury formula for inverse matrices to obtain that

\displaystyle~~\Omega_{\alpha}^{-1}-\Omega_{0}^{-1}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\left(U_{\alpha}^{-{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}}\Lambda_{n}(\alpha)U_{\alpha}^{-1}+\tau_{0}I_{n}\right)^{-1}-\Omega_{0}^{-1}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\left\{U_{\alpha}^{-{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}}\left(\Lambda_{n}(\alpha)-I_{n}\right)U_{\alpha}^{-1}+\Omega_{0}\right\}^{-1}-\Omega_{0}^{-1}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\Omega_{0}^{-1}-\Omega_{0}^{-1}U_{\alpha}^{-{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}}\left[\left\{\Lambda_{n}(\alpha)-I_{n}\right\}^{-1}+U_{\alpha}^{-1}\Omega_{0}^{-1}U_{\alpha}^{-{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}}\right]^{-1}U_{\alpha}^{-1}\Omega_{0}^{-1}-\Omega_{0}^{-1}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\Omega_{0}^{-1}U_{\alpha}^{-{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}}\left[\left\{I_{n}-\Lambda_{n}(\alpha)\right\}^{-1}-U_{\alpha}^{-1}\Omega_{0}^{-1}U_{\alpha}^{-{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}}\right]^{-1}U_{\alpha}^{-1}\Omega_{0}^{-1}.

(S.26)

Next we focus on the matrix
T_{\alpha}\equiv\left[\left\{I_{n}-\Lambda_{n}(\alpha)\right\}^{-1}-U_{\alpha}^{-1}\Omega_{0}^{-1}U_{\alpha}^{-{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}}\right]^{-1}
. Obviously
U_{\alpha}^{-1}\Omega_{0}^{-1}U_{\alpha}^{-{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}}
is positive definite. Using the definition of
\Omega_{0}
in (
S.24
), it follows from Lemma
S.7
Part (i) that

\displaystyle 0_{n\times n}\prec U_{\alpha}^{-1}\Omega_{0}^{-1}U_{\alpha}^{-{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}}

\displaystyle=U_{\alpha}^{-1}\left(U_{\alpha}^{-{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}}U_{\alpha}^{-1}+\tau_{0}I_{n}\right)^{-1}U_{\alpha}^{-{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}}

\displaystyle\preceq U_{\alpha}^{-1}\left(U_{\alpha}^{-{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}}U_{\alpha}^{-1}\right)^{-1}U_{\alpha}^{-{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}}=I_{n}.

(S.27)

Therefore, all the eigenvalues of
U_{\alpha}^{-1}\Omega_{0}^{-1}U_{\alpha}^{-{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}}
are between 0 and 1. Meanwhile, if
\alpha>\alpha_{0}
, then by Lemma
S.3
,
K_{\alpha,\nu}(S_{n})\preceq K_{\alpha_{0},\nu}(S_{n})
, and hence
\Omega_{\alpha}^{-1}\succeq\Omega_{0}^{-1}
. From (
S2.3
), this implies that
T_{\alpha}
is positive definite when
\alpha>\alpha_{0}
. Similarly,
T_{\alpha}
is negative definite when
\alpha<\alpha_{0}
. Let
T_{\alpha}=P_{\alpha}^{-1}\diag\{a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}\}P_{\alpha}
be the eigendecomposition of
T_{\alpha}
, where
P_{\alpha}
is an
n\times n
orthogonal matrix consisting of eigenvectors, and
a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}
are the eigenvalues of
T_{\alpha}
. Then
a_{1}^{-1},\ldots,a_{n}^{-1}
are the eigenvalues of
T_{\alpha}^{-1}
. When
\alpha>\alpha_{0}
, since
T_{\alpha}^{-1},\left\{I_{n}-\Lambda_{n}(\alpha)\right\}^{-1},U_{\alpha}^{-1}\Omega_{0}^{-1}U_{\alpha}^{-{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}}
are all positive definite, by applying the Weyl’s inequality to the relation
T_{\alpha}^{-1}=\left\{I_{n}-\Lambda_{n}(\alpha)\right\}^{-1}-U_{\alpha}^{-1}\Omega_{0}^{-1}U_{\alpha}^{-{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}}
, we have that up to a permutation of
a_{k}
’s,
\{1-\lambda_{k,n}(\alpha)\}^{-1}-1\leq a_{k}^{-1}\leq\{1-\lambda_{k,n}(\alpha)\}^{-1}
for
k=1,\ldots,n
. Similarly, when
\alpha<\alpha_{0}
,
T_{\alpha}^{-1}
is negative definite, and
\left\{I_{n}-\Lambda_{n}(\alpha)\right\}^{-1}
is also negative definite since all
\lambda_{k,n}(\alpha)\geq 1
by Lemma
S.2
. We can apply the Weyl’s inequality to the relation
(-T_{\alpha})^{-1}=\left\{\Lambda_{n}(\alpha)-I_{n}\right\}^{-1}+U_{\alpha}^{-1}\Omega_{0}^{-1}U_{\alpha}^{-{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}}
to obtain that
\{\lambda_{k,n}(\alpha)-1\}^{-1}\leq-a_{k}^{-1}\leq\{\lambda_{k,n}(\alpha)-1\}^{-1}+1
, or equivalently
\{1-\lambda_{k,n}(\alpha)\}^{-1}-1\leq a_{k}^{-1}\leq\{1-\lambda_{k,n}(\alpha)\}^{-1}
for
k=1,\ldots,n
.

On the other hand, from Lemma
S.2
, we have that uniformly over all
\alpha\in\Acal_{n}
, for all sufficiently large
n
,
\max_{1\leq k\leq n}|\lambda_{k,n}(\alpha)-1|\leq Ln^{-2}<1/2
, i.e.,
\{1-\lambda_{k,n}(\alpha)\}^{-1}\in[-\infty,-2]\cup[2,+\infty]
for all
k=1,\ldots,n
. Therefore, we have that for both the cases of
\alpha>\alpha_{0}
and
\alpha<\alpha_{0}
, the eigenvalues of
T_{\alpha}
satisfy

\displaystyle|a_{k}|\leq\max\left\{\left|\frac{1}{\{1-\lambda_{k,n}(\alpha)\}^{-1}}\right|,\left|\frac{1}{\{1-\lambda_{k,n}(\alpha)\}^{-1}-1}\right|\right\}

\displaystyle=\max\left\{|\lambda_{k,n}(\alpha)-1|,\left|\lambda_{k,n}(\alpha)^{-1}-1\right|\right\}\leq|\lambda_{k,n}(\alpha)-1|+\left|\lambda_{k,n}(\alpha)^{-1}-1\right|,

(S.28)

for
k=1,\ldots,n
. This also holds for the case
\alpha=\alpha_{0}
since both sides of (
S2.3
) are equal to zero.

We define the
n\times n
diagonal matrix
Q_{n}(\alpha)
:

\displaystyle Q_{n}(\alpha)

\displaystyle=\diag\left\{|\lambda_{1,n}(\alpha)-1|+\left|\lambda_{1,n}(\alpha)^{-1}-1\right|,\ldots,|\lambda_{n,n}(\alpha)-1|+\left|\lambda_{n,n}(\alpha)^{-1}-1\right|\right\}.

(S.29)

Then by Lemma
S.7
Part (i), (
S2.3
) and the eigendecomposition
T_{\alpha}=P_{\alpha}^{-1}\diag\{a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}\}P_{\alpha}
imply that

\displaystyle T_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=\left[\left\{I_{n}-\Lambda_{n}(\alpha)\right\}^{-1}-U_{\alpha}^{-1}\Omega_{0}^{-1}U_{\alpha}^{-{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}}\right]^{-1}\preceq P_{\alpha}^{-1}Q_{n}(\alpha)P_{\alpha}.

This together with (
S2.3
) implies that for all
\alpha\in\Acal_{n}
,

\displaystyle\Omega_{\alpha}^{-1}-\Omega_{0}^{-1}\preceq\Omega_{0}^{-1}U_{\alpha}^{-{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}}P_{\alpha}^{-1}Q_{n}(\alpha)P_{\alpha}U_{\alpha}^{-1}\Omega_{0}^{-1}.

Hence the first term in (
S2.3
) can be upper bounded by

\displaystyle(1-\epsilon_{1\vee 2})^{-1}\left[\tr\left\{\left(\theta_{0}K_{\alpha,\nu}(S_{n})+\tau_{0}I_{n}\right)^{-1}\widetilde{Y}_{n}\widetilde{Y}_{n}^{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}\right\}-\tr\left\{\left(\theta_{0}K_{\alpha_{0},\nu}(S_{n})+\tau_{0}I_{n}\right)^{-1}\widetilde{Y}_{n}\widetilde{Y}_{n}^{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}\right\}\right]

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle(1-\epsilon_{1\vee 2})^{-1}\widetilde{Y}_{n}^{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}\Omega_{0}^{-1}U_{\alpha}^{-{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}}P_{\alpha}^{-1}Q_{n}(\alpha)P_{\alpha}U_{\alpha}^{-1}\Omega_{0}^{-1}\widetilde{Y}_{n}.

(S.30)

Since
\Omega_{0}
is positive definite, there exists an invertible matrix
V_{0}
such that
V_{0}^{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}\Omega_{0}V_{0}=I_{n}
(
V_{0}
depends on
\alpha_{0}
and
\tau_{0}
) and hence
\Omega_{0}^{-1}=V_{0}V_{0}^{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}
. Define the random vector
Z_{n}=V_{0}^{{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}}\widetilde{Y}_{n}
. Since
\widetilde{Y}_{n}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Omega_{0})
, we have that
Z_{n}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{n})
. Therefore, we can rewrite the right-hand side of (
S2.3
) as

\displaystyle\widetilde{Y}_{n}^{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}\Omega_{0}^{-1}U_{\alpha}^{-{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}}P_{\alpha}^{-1}Q_{n}(\alpha)P_{\alpha}\Omega_{0}^{-1}\widetilde{Y}_{n}=Z_{n}^{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}V_{0}^{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}U_{\alpha}^{-{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}}P_{\alpha}^{-1}Q_{n}(\alpha)P_{\alpha}U_{\alpha}^{-1}V_{0}Z_{n}.

(S.31)

Then we apply the Hanson-Wright inequality in Lemma
S.8
to the quadratic form in (
S.31
) to obtain that for any number
z>0
and any
\alpha\in\Acal_{n}
,

\displaystyle\pr\left\{Z_{n}^{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}\Sigma_{\alpha}Z_{n}\geq\tr(\Sigma_{\alpha})+2\left\{\tr(\Sigma_{\alpha}^{2})z\right\}^{1/2}+2\|\Sigma_{\alpha}\|_{\op}z\right\}\leq\mathrm{e}^{-z},

(S.32)

where we define
\Sigma_{\alpha}=V_{0}^{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}U_{\alpha}^{-{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}}P_{\alpha}^{-1}Q_{n}(\alpha)P_{\alpha}U_{\alpha}^{-1}V_{0}
.

We continue to find upper bounds for each term related to
\Sigma_{\alpha}
in (
S.32
), using the definition of
Q_{n}(\alpha)
in (
S.29
):

\displaystyle\tr(\Sigma_{\alpha})

\displaystyle=\tr\left\{V_{0}^{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}U_{\alpha}^{-{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}}P_{\alpha}^{-1}Q_{n}(\alpha)P_{\alpha}U_{\alpha}^{-1}V_{0}\right\}=\tr\left\{P_{\alpha}^{-1}Q_{n}(\alpha)P_{\alpha}U_{\alpha}^{-1}V_{0}V_{0}^{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}U_{\alpha}^{-{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}}\right\}

\displaystyle=\tr\left\{P_{\alpha}^{-1}Q_{n}(\alpha)P_{\alpha}U_{\alpha}^{-1}\Omega_{0}^{-1}U_{\alpha}^{-{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}}\right\}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(i)}}{{\leq}}\tr\left\{P_{\alpha}^{-1}Q_{n}(\alpha)P_{\alpha}\right\}=\tr\left\{Q_{n}(\alpha)\right\}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{n}|\lambda_{1}(\alpha)-1|+\sum_{k=1}^{n}\left|\lambda_{1}(\alpha)^{-1}-1\right|.

(S.33)

\displaystyle\tr(\Sigma_{\alpha}^{2})

\displaystyle=\tr\left[\left\{V_{0}^{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}U_{\alpha}^{-{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}}P_{\alpha}^{-1}Q_{n}(\alpha)P_{\alpha}U_{\alpha}^{-1}V_{0}\right\}^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\tr\left\{V_{0}^{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}U_{\alpha}^{-{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}}P_{\alpha}^{-1}Q_{n}(\alpha)P_{\alpha}U_{\alpha}^{-1}V_{0}V_{0}^{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}U_{\alpha}^{-{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}}P_{\alpha}^{-1}Q_{n}(\alpha)P_{\alpha}U_{\alpha}^{-1}V_{0}\right\}

\displaystyle=\tr\left\{P_{\alpha}^{-1}Q_{n}(\alpha)P_{\alpha}U_{\alpha}^{-1}\Omega_{0}^{-1}U_{\alpha}^{-{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}}P_{\alpha}^{-1}Q_{n}(\alpha)P_{\alpha}U_{\alpha}^{-1}\Omega_{0}^{-1}U_{\alpha}^{-{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}}\right\}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(ii)}}{{\leq}}\tr\left\{P_{\alpha}^{-1}Q_{n}(\alpha)P_{\alpha}U_{\alpha}^{-1}\Omega_{0}^{-1}U_{\alpha}^{-{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}}P_{\alpha}^{-1}Q_{n}(\alpha)P_{\alpha}\right\}

\displaystyle=\tr\left\{P_{\alpha}^{-1}Q_{n}(\alpha)^{2}P_{\alpha}U_{\alpha}^{-1}\Omega_{0}^{-1}U_{\alpha}^{-{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}}\right\}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(iii)}}{{\leq}}\tr\left\{P_{\alpha}^{-1}Q_{n}(\alpha)^{2}P_{\alpha}\right\}=\tr\left\{Q_{n}(\alpha)^{2}\right\}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\left\{\left|\lambda_{k,n}(\alpha)-1\right|+\left|\lambda_{k,n}(\alpha)^{-1}-1\right|\right\}^{2}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(iv)}}{{\leq}}2\sum_{k=1}^{n}\left|\lambda_{k,n}(\alpha)-1\right|^{2}+2\sum_{k=1}^{n}\left|\lambda_{k,n}(\alpha)^{-1}-1\right|^{2},

(S.34)

\displaystyle\|\Sigma_{\alpha}\|_{\op}^{2}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{s}_{\max}\left[\left\{V_{0}^{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}U_{\alpha}^{-{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}}P_{\alpha}^{-1}Q_{n}(\alpha)P_{\alpha}U_{\alpha}^{-1}V_{0}\right\}^{2}\right]\leq\tr\left[\left\{V_{0}^{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}U_{\alpha}^{-{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}}P_{\alpha}^{-1}Q_{n}(\alpha)P_{\alpha}U_{\alpha}^{-1}V_{0}\right\}^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq 2\sum_{k=1}^{n}\left|\lambda_{k,n}(\alpha)-1\right|^{2}+2\sum_{k=1}^{n}\left|\lambda_{k,n}(\alpha)^{-1}-1\right|^{2},

(S.35)

where (i), (ii) and (iii) follow from Lemma
S.7
and (
S2.3
), (iv) follows from the inequality
(a+b)^{2}\leq 2(a^{2}+b^{2})
for any
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
.

If we take
z=(\log n)^{2}
, using the upper bounds in Lemma
S.4
(noticing that
\Acal_{n}^{\dagger}
and
\Acal_{n}
have the same condition on
\alpha
), we can obtain from (
S.33
)-(
S.35
) that for all sufficiently large
n
,

\displaystyle\sup_{\alpha\in\Acal_{n}}\left\{\tr(\Sigma_{\alpha})+2\left\{\tr(\Sigma_{\alpha}^{2})\right\}^{1/2}\log n+2\|\Sigma_{\alpha}\|_{\op}\log^{2}n\right\}

\displaystyle\leq Ln^{-1}+2Ln^{-1}+4\left(L^{2}n^{-3}+4L^{2}n^{-3}\right)^{1/2}\log n+4\left(L^{2}n^{-3}+4L^{2}n^{-3}\right)^{1/2}\log^{2}n\leq 4Ln^{-1}.

(S.36)

Because the matrix
K_{\alpha,\nu}(S_{n})
is non-increasing in
\alpha
by Lemma
S.3
, we can see from the definition of
\Omega_{\alpha}
in (
S.24
) that the first term in (
S2.3
) is an increasing function in
\alpha
. Let
\overline{\alpha}_{n}=\alpha_{0}(1+n^{-2/\kappa})
. Then the first term in (
S2.3
) attains its supremum at
\overline{\alpha}_{n}
. We combine (
S2.3
), (
S.31
), (
S.32
) and (
S2.3
) to obtain that

\displaystyle\pr\Bigg\{\sup_{\Acal_{n}}(1-\epsilon_{1\vee 2})^{-1}\Big[\tr\left\{\left(\theta_{0}K_{\alpha,\nu}(S_{n})+\tau_{0}I_{n}\right)^{-1}\widetilde{Y}_{n}\widetilde{Y}_{n}^{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}\right\}

\displaystyle\quad-\tr\left\{\left(\theta_{0}K_{\alpha_{0},\nu}(S_{n})+\tau_{0}I_{n}\right)^{-1}\widetilde{Y}_{n}\widetilde{Y}_{n}^{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}\right\}\Big]\geq(1-\epsilon_{1\vee 2})^{-1}4Ln^{-1}\Bigg\}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\pr\Bigg\{(1-\epsilon_{1\vee 2})^{-1}\Big(\tr\left[\left\{\theta_{0}K_{\overline{\alpha}_{n},\nu}(S_{n})+\tau_{0}I_{n}\right\}^{-1}\widetilde{Y}_{n}\widetilde{Y}_{n}^{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}\right]

\displaystyle\quad-\tr\left\{\left(\theta_{0}K_{\alpha_{0},\nu}(S_{n})+\tau_{0}I_{n}\right)^{-1}\widetilde{Y}_{n}\widetilde{Y}_{n}^{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}\right\}\Big)\geq(1-\epsilon_{1\vee 2})^{-1}4Ln^{-1}\Bigg\}

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle\pr\Bigg\{\widetilde{Y}_{n}^{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}\Omega_{0}^{-1}U_{\overline{\alpha}_{n}}^{-{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}}P_{\overline{\alpha}_{n}}^{-1}Q_{n}(\overline{\alpha}_{n})P_{\overline{\alpha}_{n}}U_{\overline{\alpha}_{n}}^{-1}\Omega_{0}^{-1}\widetilde{Y}_{n}\geq 4Ln^{-1}\Bigg\}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\pr\Bigg\{Z_{n}^{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}\Sigma_{\overline{\alpha}_{n}}Z_{n}\geq 4Ln^{-1}\Bigg\}

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle\pr\Bigg\{Z_{n}^{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}\Sigma_{\overline{\alpha}_{n}}Z_{n}\geq\tr(\Sigma_{\overline{\alpha}_{n}})+2\left\{\tr(\Sigma_{\overline{\alpha}_{n}}^{2})\right\}^{1/2}\log n+2\|\Sigma_{\overline{\alpha}_{n}}\|_{\op}\log^{2}n\Bigg\}

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle\exp(-\log^{2}n).

(S.37)

For the second term on the right-hand side of (
S2.3
), with the relation
\tr\left(\Omega_{0}^{-1}\widetilde{Y}_{n}\widetilde{Y}_{n}^{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}\right)=Z_{n}^{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}Z_{n}
, we apply the Hanson-Wright inequality in Lemma
S.8
with
\Sigma=I_{n}
and
z=(\log n)^{2}
to obtain that for all sufficiently large
n
,

\displaystyle\quad~\pr\left\{\frac{\epsilon_{1\vee 2}}{1-\epsilon_{1\vee 2}}\tr\left(\Omega_{0}^{-1}\widetilde{Y}_{n}\widetilde{Y}_{n}^{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}\right)\geq\frac{\epsilon_{1\vee 2}}{1-\epsilon_{1\vee 2}}\cdot 2n\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\pr\left\{Z_{n}^{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}Z_{n}\geq n+2\sqrt{n}\log n+2\log^{2}n\right\}\leq\exp(-\log^{2}n).

(S.38)

Lower bound for the second term in (
S.5
):

We first show a simple fact related to the Kullback-Leibler (KL) divergence. For two generic probability distributions
F_{1},F_{2}
with densities
f_{1},f_{2}
, the KL divergence from
F_{1}
to
F_{2}
is defined as
\kl(F_{1},F_{2})=\int f_{1}\log(f_{1}/f_{2})
. The KL divergence from
\mathcal{N}\left(0,\theta K_{\alpha,\nu}(S_{n})+\tau I_{n}\right)
to

\mathcal{N}\left(0,\theta_{0}K_{\alpha_{0},\nu}(S_{n})+\tau_{0}I_{n}\right)
is

\displaystyle\kl\left(\mathcal{N}\left(0,\theta K_{\alpha,\nu}(S_{n})+\tau I_{n}\right),\mathcal{N}\left(0,\theta_{0}K_{\alpha_{0},\nu}(S_{n})+\tau_{0}I_{n}\right)\right)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\left[\log\frac{\dett\left(\theta_{0}K_{\alpha_{0},\nu}(S_{n})+\tau_{0}I_{n}\right)}{\dett\left(\theta K_{\alpha,\nu}(S_{n})+\tau I_{n}\right)}+\tr\left\{\left(\theta_{0}K_{\alpha_{0},\nu}(S_{n})+\tau_{0}I_{n}\right)^{-1}\left(\theta K_{\alpha,\nu}(S_{n})+\tau I_{n}\right)\right\}-n\right].

Since the KL divergence is always nonnegative, this implies that

\displaystyle\log\frac{\dett\left\{\theta K_{\alpha,\nu}(S_{n})+\tau I_{n}\right\}}{\dett\left\{\theta_{0}K_{\alpha_{0},\nu}(S_{n})+\tau_{0}I_{n}\right\}}\leq\tr\left[\left\{\theta_{0}K_{\alpha_{0},\nu}(S_{n})+\tau_{0}I_{n}\right\}^{-1}\left\{\theta K_{\alpha,\nu}(S_{n})+\tau I_{n}\right\}\right]-n.

(S.39)

On the parameter set
\Bcal_{0}(\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2})
,
\theta\leq(1+\epsilon_{1})\theta_{0}
and
\tau\leq(1+\epsilon_{2})\tau_{0}
. Therefore, we have
\theta K_{\alpha,\nu}(S_{n})+\tau I_{n}\preceq(1+\epsilon_{1\vee 2})\left(\theta_{0}K_{\alpha,\nu}(S_{n})+\tau_{0}I_{n}\right)
.
By (
S.39
) and Lemma
S.7
, we have that

\displaystyle\quad\log\frac{\dett\left\{\theta K_{\alpha,\nu}(S_{n})+\tau I_{n}\right\}}{\dett\left\{\theta_{0}K_{\alpha_{0},\nu}(S_{n})+\tau_{0}I_{n}\right\}}

\displaystyle\leq\tr\left[\left\{\theta_{0}K_{\alpha_{0},\nu}(S_{n})+\tau_{0}I_{n}\right\}^{-1}\left\{\theta K_{\alpha,\nu}(S_{n})+\tau I_{n}\right\}\right]-n

\displaystyle\leq\tr\left[\left\{\theta_{0}K_{\alpha_{0},\nu}(S_{n})+\tau_{0}I_{n}\right\}^{-1}\left\{(1+\epsilon_{1\vee 2})\theta_{0}K_{\alpha,\nu}(S_{n})+(1+\epsilon_{1\vee 2})\tau_{0}I_{n}\right\}\right]-n

\displaystyle=(1+\epsilon_{1\vee 2})\tr\left[\left\{\theta_{0}K_{\alpha_{0},\nu}(S_{n})+\tau_{0}I_{n}\right\}^{-1}\left\{\theta_{0}K_{\alpha,\nu}(S_{n})+\tau_{0}I_{n}\right\}\right]-n

\displaystyle=(1+\epsilon_{1\vee 2})\theta_{0}\tr\left[\left\{\theta_{0}K_{\alpha_{0},\nu}(S_{n})+\tau_{0}I_{n}\right\}^{-1}\left\{K_{\alpha,\nu}(S_{n})-K_{\alpha_{0},\nu}(S_{n})\right\}\right]

\displaystyle\quad+(1+\epsilon_{1\vee 2})\tr\left[\left\{\theta_{0}K_{\alpha_{0},\nu}(S_{n})+\tau_{0}I_{n}\right\}^{-1}\left\{\theta_{0}K_{\alpha_{0},\nu}(S_{n})+\tau_{0}I_{n}\right\}\right]-n

\displaystyle=(1+\epsilon_{1\vee 2})\theta_{0}\tr\left[\left\{\theta_{0}K_{\alpha_{0},\nu}(S_{n})+\tau_{0}I_{n}\right\}^{-1}\left\{K_{\alpha,\nu}(S_{n})-K_{\alpha_{0},\nu}(S_{n})\right\}\right]+n\epsilon_{1\vee 2}.

(S.40)

We analyze the first term on the right-hand side of (
S2.3
). By Lemma
S.2
and Lemma
S.7
, for all
|\alpha/\alpha_{0}-1|\leq n^{-2/\kappa}
, for all sufficiently large
n
,

\displaystyle\theta_{0}\left\{K_{\alpha,\nu}(S_{n})-K_{\alpha_{0},\nu}(S_{n})\right\}=U_{\alpha}^{-{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}}\left\{\Lambda_{n}(\alpha)-I_{n}\right\}U_{\alpha}^{-1}

\displaystyle\preceq\sup_{\Acal_{n}}\max_{1\leq k\leq n}|\lambda_{k,n}(\alpha)-1|\cdot U_{\alpha}^{-{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}}U_{\alpha}^{-1}\preceq(Ln^{-2})U_{\alpha}^{-{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}}U_{\alpha}^{-1}.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\theta_{0}\tr\left\{\left(\theta_{0}K_{\alpha_{0},\nu}(S_{n})+\tau_{0}I_{n}\right)^{-1}\left(K_{\alpha,\nu}(S_{n})-K_{\alpha_{0},\nu}(S_{n})\right)\right\}

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle\tr\left\{\left(\theta_{0}K_{\alpha_{0},\nu}(S_{n})\right)^{-1}\left(\theta_{0}K_{\alpha,\nu}(S_{n})-\theta_{0}K_{\alpha_{0},\nu}(S_{n})\right)\right\}

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle\tr\left\{U_{\alpha}U_{\alpha}^{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}(Ln^{-2})U_{\alpha}^{-{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}}U_{\alpha}^{-1}\right\}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\tr\left(Ln^{-2}I_{n}\right)=Ln^{-1}.

(S.41)

We combine (
S2.3
) and (
S2.3
) to obtain that for all sufficiently large
n
,

\displaystyle\quad\sup_{\Acal_{n}}\log\frac{\dett\left(\theta K_{\alpha,\nu}(S_{n})+\tau I_{n}\right)}{\dett\left(\theta_{0}K_{\alpha_{0},\nu}(S_{n})+\tau_{0}I_{n}\right)}\leq(1+\epsilon_{1\vee 2})Ln^{-1}+n\epsilon_{1\vee 2}.

(S.42)

Finally, we combine (
S2.3
), (
S2.3
), (
S2.3
) and (
S.42
) to obtain that with probability for all sufficiently large
n
, at least
1-2\exp(-\log^{2}n)
,

\displaystyle\quad\inf_{\Bcal_{0}(\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2})\cap\Acal_{n}}\Bigg\{-\frac{1}{2}\Big(\tr\left[\left\{\theta K_{\alpha,\nu}(S_{n})+\tau I_{n}\right\}^{-1}\widetilde{Y}_{n}\widetilde{Y}_{n}^{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}\right]

\displaystyle\qquad-\tr\left[\left\{\theta_{0}K_{\alpha_{0},\nu}(S_{n})+\tau_{0}I_{n}\right\}^{-1}\widetilde{Y}_{n}\widetilde{Y}_{n}^{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}\right]\Big)-\frac{1}{2}\log\frac{\dett\left\{\theta K_{\alpha,\nu}(S_{n})+\tau I_{n}\right\}}{\dett\left\{\theta_{0}K_{\alpha_{0},\nu}(S_{n})+\tau_{0}I_{n}\right\}}\Bigg\}

\displaystyle\geq-\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{4Ln^{-1}}{1-\epsilon_{1\vee 2}}+\frac{2n\epsilon_{1\vee 2}}{1-\epsilon_{1\vee 2}}+(1+\epsilon_{1\vee 2})Ln^{-1}+n\epsilon_{1\vee 2}\right)

\displaystyle\geq-\left(\frac{5}{2}n\epsilon_{1\vee 2}+5Ln^{-1}\right),

where the last inequality follows since
\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}\in(0,1/2)
.
∎

Lemma S.6
.

Suppose that Assumptions
1
and
2
hold. Let
\Dcal_{n}=\big\{(\theta,\alpha,\tau,\beta)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{p}:

\|\beta-\beta_{0}\|\leq n^{-3}\big\}
. There exists a large integer
N_{4}
that
\nu,d,\theta_{0},\alpha_{0},L,r_{0},\kappa
, such that for any
\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}\in(0,1/2)
, for all
n>N_{4}
, with probability at least
1-\exp(-\log^{2}n)
,

\displaystyle\inf_{\Bcal_{0}(\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2})\cap\Dcal_{n}}\Big\{\widetilde{Y}_{n}^{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}\left[\left\{\theta K_{\alpha,\nu}(S_{n})+\tau I_{n}\right\}^{-1}-\left\{\theta_{0}K_{\alpha_{0},\nu}(S_{n})+\tau_{0}I_{n}\right\}^{-1}\right]F_{n}(\beta-\beta_{0})

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{1}{2}(\beta-\beta_{0})^{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}F_{n}^{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}\left[\left\{\theta K_{\alpha,\nu}(S_{n})+\tau I_{n}\right\}^{-1}-\left\{\theta_{0}K_{\alpha_{0},\nu}(S_{n})+\tau_{0}I_{n}\right\}^{-1}\right]F_{n}(\beta-\beta_{0})\Big\}

\displaystyle\geq-8C_{\mathrm{f}}p^{1/2}\left\{\theta_{0}K_{\alpha_{0},\nu}(0)+\tau_{0}\right\}^{1/2}\tau_{0}^{-1}n^{-1}-2C_{\mathrm{f}}^{2}p\tau_{0}^{-1}n^{-4}.

(S.43)

Proof of
Lemma
S.6
.

We derive lower bounds for each of the two terms inside the infimum of (
S.6
). On the event
\Dcal_{n}
,
\|\beta-\beta_{0}\|\leq n^{-3}
. By Assumption
1
, we have that

\displaystyle\|F_{n}(\beta-\beta_{0})\|\leq\|F_{n}\|_{F}\|\beta-\beta_{0}\|\leq C_{\mathrm{f}}p^{1/2}n^{1/2}\|\beta-\beta_{0}\|\leq C_{\mathrm{f}}p^{1/2}n^{-2}.

(S.44)

On the set
\Bcal_{0}(\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2})
, since both matrices
\theta K_{\alpha,\nu}(S_{n})+\tau I_{n}
and
\theta_{0}K_{\alpha_{0},\nu}(S_{n})+\tau_{0}I_{n}
are positive definite, we apply the triangle inequality for the matrix operator norm and obtain that

\displaystyle\quad~\left\|\{\theta K_{\alpha,\nu}(S_{n})+\tau I_{n}\}^{-1}-\{\theta_{0}K_{\alpha_{0},\nu}(S_{n})+\tau_{0}I_{n}\}^{-1}\right\|_{\op}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\{\theta K_{\alpha,\nu}(S_{n})+\tau I_{n}\}^{-1}\right\|_{\op}+\left\|\{\theta_{0}K_{\alpha_{0},\nu}(S_{n})+\tau_{0}I_{n}\}^{-1}\right\|_{\op}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\tau}+\frac{1}{\tau_{0}}\leq\frac{2}{(1-\epsilon_{2})\tau_{0}}.

(S.45)

Since
\widetilde{Y}_{n}\sim\Ncal(0,\Omega_{0})
, similar to the proof of Lemma
S.5
, we let
V_{0}
be an invertible matrix such that
V_{0}^{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}\Omega_{0}V_{0}=I_{n}
and let
Z_{n}=V_{0}^{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}\widetilde{Y}_{n}
such that
Z_{n}\sim\Ncal(0,I_{n})
. Then by applying the Hanson-Wright inequality in Lemma
S.8
, we have that for all sufficiently large
n
,

\displaystyle\Pr\left(\|V_{0}^{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}\widetilde{Y}_{n}\|\geq\sqrt{2n}\right)=\Pr\left(\|Z_{n}\|^{2}\geq 2n\right)

\displaystyle\leq\Pr\left(Z_{n}^{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}Z_{n}\geq n+2\sqrt{n}\log n+2\log^{2}n\right)\leq\exp(-\log^{2}n).

(S.46)

Notice that since
V_{0}^{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}\Omega_{0}V_{0}=I_{n}
, we have that
V_{0}^{-{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}}V_{0}^{-1}=\Omega_{0}
and hence

\displaystyle\|V_{0}^{-1}\|_{\op}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\|\Omega_{0}\|_{\op}\leq\left\|\theta_{0}K_{\alpha_{0},\nu}(S_{n})+\tau_{0}I_{n}\right\|_{\op}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\theta_{0}K_{\alpha_{0},\nu}(S_{n})\right\|_{\op}+\|\tau_{0}I_{n}\|_{\op}\leq\theta_{0}\tr\left\{K_{\alpha_{0},\nu}(S_{n})\right\}+\tau_{0}

\displaystyle\leq n\theta_{0}K_{\alpha_{0},\nu}(0)+\tau_{0},

(S.47)

where
K_{\alpha_{0},\nu}(0)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
is a constant since
K_{\alpha_{0},\nu}(\mathbf{s}-\mathbf{t})
is a covariance function for
\mathbf{s},\mathbf{t}\in[0,1]^{d}
.

We combine (
S.44
), (
S2.3
), (
S2.3
), and (
S2.3
) to conclude that with probability at least
1-\exp(-\log^{2}n)
, on the event
\Bcal_{0}(\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2})\cap\Dcal_{n}
,

\displaystyle~\quad\widetilde{Y}_{n}^{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}\left[\left\{\theta K_{\alpha,\nu}(S_{n})+\tau I_{n}\right\}^{-1}-\left\{\theta_{0}K_{\alpha_{0},\nu}(S_{n})+\tau_{0}I_{n}\right\}^{-1}\right]F_{n}(\beta-\beta_{0})

\displaystyle\geq-\|\widetilde{Y}_{n}^{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}V_{0}\|\cdot\|V_{0}^{-1}\|_{\op}\cdot\left\|\left\{\theta K_{\alpha,\nu}(S_{n})+\tau I_{n}\right\}^{-1}-\left\{\theta_{0}K_{\alpha_{0},\nu}(S_{n})+\tau_{0}I_{n}\right\}^{-1}\right\|_{\op}\cdot\|F_{n}(\beta-\beta_{0})\|

\displaystyle\geq-\left[2n\cdot\left\{n\theta_{0}K_{\alpha_{0},\nu}(0)+\tau_{0}\right\}\right]^{1/2}\cdot\frac{2}{(1-\epsilon_{2})\tau_{0}}\cdot C_{\mathrm{f}}p^{1/2}n^{-2}

\displaystyle\geq-8C_{\mathrm{f}}p^{1/2}\left\{\theta_{0}K_{\alpha_{0},\nu}(0)+\tau_{0}\right\}^{1/2}\tau_{0}^{-1}n^{-1},

(S.48)

for all sufficiently large
n
, where in the last inequality we used
\epsilon_{2}<1/2
.

We can also derive from (
S.44
) and (
S2.3
) that on the event
\Bcal_{0}(\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2})\cap\Dcal_{n}
,

\displaystyle~\quad-\frac{1}{2}(\beta-\beta_{0})^{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}F_{n}^{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}\left[\left\{\theta K_{\alpha,\nu}(S_{n})+\tau I_{n}\right\}^{-1}-\left\{\theta_{0}K_{\alpha_{0},\nu}(S_{n})+\tau_{0}I_{n}\right\}^{-1}\right]F_{n}(\beta-\beta_{0})

\displaystyle\geq-\frac{1}{2}\left\|\left\{\theta K_{\alpha,\nu}(S_{n})+\tau I_{n}\right\}^{-1}-\left\{\theta_{0}K_{\alpha_{0},\nu}(S_{n})+\tau_{0}I_{n}\right\}^{-1}\right\|_{\op}\cdot\|F_{n}(\beta-\beta_{0})\|^{2}

\displaystyle\geq-\frac{1}{2}\cdot\frac{2}{(1-\epsilon_{2})\tau_{0}}\cdot\left(C_{\mathrm{f}}p^{1/2}n^{-2}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\geq-\frac{2C_{\mathrm{f}}^{2}p}{\tau_{0}}n^{-4},

(S.49)

where in the last inequality we used
\epsilon_{2}<1/2
. The conclusion of Lemma
S.6
follows from (
S2.3
) and (
S2.3
).
∎

S2.4
Other Technical Lemmas

Lemma S.7
.

The following results hold for any
n\times n
symmetric matrices
A_{1},A_{2}
.

(i)

If
A_{1}\preceq A_{2}
, then for any
n\times n
matrix
B
,
B^{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}A_{1}B\preceq B^{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}A_{2}B
;

(ii)

If
A_{1}\preceq A_{2}
, then for any positive semidefinite matrix
B
,
\tr(BA_{1})\leq\tr(BA_{2})
and
\tr(A_{1}B)\leq\tr(A_{2}B)
.

Proof of
Lemma
S.7
.

(i) If
A_{1}\preceq A_{2}
, then
A_{2}-A_{1}
is positive semidefinite, which means that
B^{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}(A_{2}-A_{1})B
is positive semidefinite. Therefore,
B^{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}A_{1}B\preceq B^{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}A_{1}B+B^{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}(A_{2}-A_{1})B=B^{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}A_{2}B
.

(ii) If
A_{1}\preceq A_{2}
, then
A_{2}-A_{1}
is positive semidefinite, and

\displaystyle\tr(BA_{2})-\tr(BA_{1})

\displaystyle=\tr\left\{B(A_{2}-A_{1})\right\}=\tr\left\{B^{1/2}(A_{2}-A_{1})B^{1/2}\right\}\geq 0,

\displaystyle\tr(A_{2}B)-\tr(A_{1}B)

\displaystyle=\tr\left\{(A_{2}-A_{1})B\right\}=\tr\left\{B^{1/2}(A_{2}-A_{1})B^{1/2}\right\}\geq 0,

where
B^{1/2}
denotes the symmetric square root matrix of a positive semidefinite matrix
B
.
∎

Lemma S.8
.

(Hanson-Wright Inequality, Proposition 1.1 in
Hsu
et al. 2012
, Theorem 1.1 in
Rudelson and
Vershynin 2013
) Let
Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{n}
be i.i.d.
\mathcal{N}(0,1)
random variables and
Z=(Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{n})^{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}
. Let
\Sigma
be a positive definite matrix. Then for any
z>0
,

\displaystyle\pr\left\{Z^{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}\Sigma Z\geq\tr(\Sigma)+2\big\{\tr(\Sigma^{2})z\big\}^{1/2}+2\|\Sigma\|_{\op}z\right\}\leq\mathrm{e}^{-z}.

Furthermore, there exists a universal absolute constant
C_{\mathrm{HW}}>0
that does not depend on
\Sigma
, such that for any
\epsilon>0
,

\displaystyle\pr\left\{\left|Z^{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}\Sigma Z-\tr(\Sigma)\right|\geq\epsilon\right\}\leq 2\exp\left(-C_{\mathrm{HW}}\min\left\{\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{\|\Sigma\|_{F}^{2}},\frac{\epsilon}{\|\Sigma\|_{\op}}\right\}\right).

S3
Posterior Inconsistency for
\alpha
and
\beta

Proposition S.1
.

Suppose that Assumptions
1
and
7
hold where the dimension of the domain
[0,1]^{d}
is
d\in\{1,2,3\}
, and
\theta K_{\alpha,\nu}
is the isotropic Matérn covariance function in Example
1
. Suppose that we assign the uniform prior on the following set which covers the true parameter
(\theta_{0},\alpha_{0},\tau_{0},\beta_{0})
:

\displaystyle\Scal_{\Pi}

\displaystyle=\Big\{(\theta,\alpha,\tau,\beta):\theta\in[\theta_{0}/2,2\theta_{0}],\alpha\in[\alpha_{0}/2,2\alpha_{0}],\tau\in[\tau_{0}/2,2\tau_{0}],

\displaystyle~~~~\beta_{j}\in[\beta_{0j}/2,2\beta_{0j}],~j=1,\ldots,p.\Big\}

Then the posterior
\Pi(\cdot\mid Y_{n},F_{n})
is inconsistent for
\alpha
and
\beta
, i.e., there exist constants
\epsilon_{0}>0,\delta_{0}>0,\eta_{0}\in(0,1)
, such that for any
N\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists
n>N
, such that

\displaystyle\Pr\left(\Pi(|\alpha-\alpha_{0}|\geq\epsilon_{0}|Y_{n},F_{n})\geq\delta_{0}\right)\geq\eta_{0},

and

\displaystyle\Pr\left(\Pi(\|\beta-\beta_{0}\|\geq\epsilon_{0}|Y_{n},F_{n})\geq\delta_{0}\right)\geq\eta_{0}.

Proof of
Proposition
S.1
.

We prove the posterior inconsistency for
\alpha
and
\beta
by contradiction. Suppose that the conclusion does not hold, i.e., for any
\epsilon>0,\delta>0,\eta\in(0,1)
, there exists a
n_{0}(\epsilon,\delta,\eta)\in\mathbb{N}
, such that for all
n>n_{0}(\epsilon,\delta,\eta)
,
\Pi(|\alpha-\alpha_{0}|\geq\epsilon|Y_{n},F_{n})<\delta
and
\Pi(\|\beta-\beta_{0}\|\geq\epsilon|Y_{n},F_{n})<\delta
with
\mathbb{P}_{(\theta_{0},\alpha_{0},\tau_{0},\beta_{0})}
-probability at least
1-\eta
. For a sufficiently small
\epsilon>0
, the uniform prior density
\pi(\theta,\alpha,\tau,\beta)
is a constant in an open neighborhood of
(\theta_{0},\alpha_{0},\tau_{0},\beta_{0})
, denoted by

\Ucal(\epsilon)=\left\{(\theta,\alpha,\tau,\beta):|\theta-\theta_{0}|<\epsilon,|\alpha-\alpha_{0}|<\epsilon,|\tau-\tau_{0}|<\epsilon,\|\beta-\beta_{0}\|_{\infty}<\epsilon/\sqrt{p}\right\},

where
\|\beta\|_{\infty}=\max_{1\leq j\leq p}|\beta_{j}|
. Under Assumption
1
, the log-likelihood function
\Lcal_{n}(\theta,\alpha,\tau,\beta)
is clearly finite for every
(\theta,\alpha,\tau,\beta)\in\Ucal(\epsilon)
. Therefore, the posterior distribution, provided exists, has a continuous density on
\Ucal(\epsilon)
. Now set
\delta=1/8
. Then with
\mathbb{P}_{(\theta_{0},\alpha_{0},\tau_{0},\beta_{0})}
-probability at least
1-\eta
, the posterior median of
\alpha
, denoted by
\widetilde{\alpha}_{n}
, is in the interval
(\alpha_{0}-\epsilon,\alpha_{0}+\epsilon)
. It follows that for any
\epsilon>0,\eta>0
, for all
n>n_{0}(\epsilon,1/8,\eta)
,
|\widetilde{\alpha}_{n}-\alpha_{0}|<\epsilon
with
\mathbb{P}_{(\theta_{0},\alpha_{0},\tau_{0},\beta_{0})}
-probability at least
1-\eta
. Thus,
\widetilde{\alpha}_{n}
is a consistent frequentist estimator for
\alpha_{0}
. Similarly, we can take the posterior median of each component
\beta_{j}
(
j=1,\ldots,p
), denoted by
\widetilde{\beta}_{nj}
, and let
\widetilde{\beta}_{n}=(\widetilde{\beta}_{n1},\ldots,\widetilde{\beta}_{np})^{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}
. Each
\widetilde{\beta}_{nj}
is a consistent frequentist estimator for
\beta_{0j}
. As a result,
\widetilde{\beta}_{n}
is a consistent frequentist estimator for
\beta_{0}
given that
\big\|\widetilde{\beta}_{n}-\beta_{0}\big\|^{2}=\sum_{j=1}^{p}\big|\widetilde{\beta}_{nj}-\beta_{0j}\big|^{2}<p\cdot(\epsilon^{2}/p)=\epsilon^{2}
. In this way, we have obtained two consistent frequentist estimators
\widetilde{\alpha}_{n}
for
\alpha_{0}
and
\widetilde{\beta}_{n}
for
\beta_{0}
, both of which only depend on the data
(Y_{n},F_{n})
.

Now we show that such consistent frequentist estimators for
\alpha
and
\beta
should not exist. By
Stein 1999
and
Zhang 2004
, the Gaussian measures induced by two Gaussian processes are either equivalent (absolutely continuous) or orthogonal to each other. For
\gp(m_{0},\theta_{0}K_{\alpha_{0},\nu})
and
\gp(m_{1},\theta_{1}K_{\alpha_{1},\nu})
with the same smoothness parameter
\nu
, the theory in Chapter 4 of
Stein 1999
(Section 4.2 and Corollary 5) and Theorem 2 of
Zhang 2004
have shown that when
d=1,2,3
, their induced Gaussian measures are equivalent to each other if: (a)
\theta_{0}=\theta_{1}
; (b) Both mean functions
m_{i}
lie in the reproducing kernel Hilbert space of
\theta_{i}K_{\alpha_{i},\nu}
for
i=0,1
. Let
L_{2}([0,1]^{d})
be the space of square integrable functions on
[0,1]^{d}
with the norm
\|\cdot\|_{2}
). Then the Sobolev space of order
k
for any
k>0
, denoted by
\Wcal_{2}^{k}([0,1]^{d})
, is
\Wcal_{2}^{k}([0,1]^{d})=\Big\{f\in L_{2}([0,1]^{d}):\|f\|^{2}_{\Wcal_{2}^{k}}=\sum_{\mathsf{j}\in\mathbb{N}^{d}:|\mathsf{j}|\leq k}\left\|\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{j}}f\right\|_{2}^{2}<\infty\Big\}
. By Assumption
7
, we have that
m(\cdot)=\beta^{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}\mathrm{f}(\cdot)
belongs to
\Wcal_{2}^{\nu+d/2}([0,1]^{d})
for any
\beta\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
, and hence belongs to the reproducing kernel Hilbert space of
\theta_{i}K_{\alpha_{i},\nu}
for
i=0,1
. By Corollary 10.48 of
Wendland 2005
, the reproducing kernel Hilbert space of the isotropic Matérn covariance function
\theta_{i}K_{\alpha_{i},\nu}
for
i=0,1
are norm equivalent to the Sobolev space of order
\nu+d/2
.

We choose
\theta_{0}=\theta_{1}
,
\alpha_{0}\neq\alpha_{1}
,
\beta_{0}\neq\beta_{1}
and let
m_{i}(\cdot)=\beta_{i}^{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}\mathrm{f}(\cdot)
for
i=0,1
. Then both the conditions (a) and (b) above hold true, such that the two Gaussian measures
\gp(m_{0},\theta_{0}K_{\alpha_{0},\nu})
and
\gp(m_{1},\theta_{1}K_{\alpha_{1},\nu})
are equivalent. Using similar argument to Corollary 1 of
Zhang 2004
, if
\widetilde{\alpha}_{n}
and
\widetilde{\beta}_{n}
are consistent for
\alpha_{0}
and
\beta_{0}
, then we can always find almost-surely convergence subsequences
\{\widetilde{\alpha}_{n_{k}}\}
and
\{\widetilde{\beta}_{n_{j}}\}
, such that

\mathbb{P}_{(\theta_{0},\alpha_{0},\tau_{0},\beta_{0})}\left(\lim_{k\to\infty}\widetilde{\alpha}_{n_{k}}=\alpha_{0}\right)=1,\text{ and }\mathbb{P}_{(\theta_{0},\alpha_{0},\tau_{0},\beta_{0})}\left(\lim_{j\to\infty}\widetilde{\beta}_{n_{j}}=\beta_{0}\right)=1.

But given the equivalence of the two Gaussian measures, this implies that

\mathbb{P}_{(\theta_{1},\alpha_{1},\tau_{1},\beta_{1})}\left(\lim_{k\to\infty}\widetilde{\alpha}_{n_{k}}=\alpha_{0}\right)=1\text{ and }\mathbb{P}_{(\theta_{1},\alpha_{1},\tau_{1},\beta_{1})}\left(\lim_{j\to\infty}\widetilde{\beta}_{n_{j}}=\beta_{0}\right)=1.

However, under
\mathbb{P}_{(\theta_{1},\alpha_{1},\tau_{1},\beta_{1})}
, the limits of
\{\widetilde{\alpha}_{n_{k}}\}
and
\{\widetilde{\beta}_{n_{j}}\}
should be
\alpha_{1}
and
\beta_{1}
, respectively, which are different from
\alpha_{0}
and
\beta_{0}
. Therefore, this is a contradiction, and such consistent estimators
\widetilde{\alpha}_{n}
and
\widetilde{\beta}_{n}
cannot exist. This further implies that the posterior
\Pi(\cdot\mid Y_{n},F_{n})
is inconsistent for
\alpha
and
\beta
.
∎

S4
Proof of Proposition
1

Proof of
Proposition
1
.

We check (i) and (ii) in Assumption
2
for the four covariance functions in Examples
1
–
4
, respectively.

Checking (i) of Assumption
2
:

Since for all
\alpha
that satisfies
|\alpha/\alpha_{0}-1|\leq r_{0}
,

\displaystyle\quad~\sup_{w\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left|f_{\theta_{0},\alpha,\nu}(w)/f_{\theta_{0},\alpha_{0},\nu}(w)-1\right|

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{w\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{\left|f_{\theta_{0},\alpha,\nu}(w)-f_{\theta_{0},\alpha_{0},\nu}(w)\right|}{f_{\theta_{0},\alpha_{0},\nu}(w)}\leq\left\{\sup_{|\alpha/\alpha_{0}-1|\leq r_{0}}\sup_{w\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\|\frac{\frac{\partial f_{\theta_{0},\alpha,\nu}(w)}{\partial\alpha}}{f_{\theta_{0},\alpha_{0},\nu}(w)}\right\|\right\}\cdot\alpha_{0}\left|\frac{\alpha}{\alpha_{0}}-1\right|,

(S.50)

we can see that it suffices to show that the double supremum in the first term is a continuous function on the set
\{\alpha:|\alpha/\alpha_{0}-1|\leq r_{0}\}
for some
r_{0}\in(0,1/2)
and is upper bounded by a constant that may depend on
\nu,d,\theta_{0},\alpha_{0}
. We can simply set
r_{0}=1/4
and
\kappa=1
.

(i) The isotropic Matérn covariance function has the following spectral density (
Stein 1999
) for any
(\theta,\alpha,\nu)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{3}
:

\displaystyle f_{\theta,\alpha,\nu}(w)=\frac{\Gamma(\nu+d/2)}{\Gamma(\nu)\pi^{d/2}}\frac{\theta}{(\alpha^{2}+\|w\|^{2})^{\nu+d/2}}.

(S.51)

Therefore,

\displaystyle\left|\frac{\frac{\partial f_{\theta_{0},\alpha,\nu}(w)}{\partial\alpha}}{f_{\theta_{0},\alpha_{0},\nu}(w)}\right|=\left|\frac{\frac{\Gamma(\nu+d/2)}{\Gamma(\nu)\pi^{d/2}}\frac{-\theta\cdot 2(\nu+d/2)\alpha}{(\alpha^{2}+\|w\|^{2})^{\nu+d/2+1}}}{\frac{\Gamma(\nu+d/2)}{\Gamma(\nu)\pi^{d/2}}\frac{\theta}{(\alpha^{2}+\|w\|^{2})^{\nu+d/2}}}\right|=\frac{2(\nu+d/2)\alpha}{\alpha^{2}+\|w\|^{2}}.

This implies that

\displaystyle\sup_{|\alpha/\alpha_{0}-1|\leq r_{0}}\sup_{w\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\|\frac{\frac{\partial f_{\theta_{0},\alpha,\nu}(w)}{\partial\alpha}}{f_{\theta_{0},\alpha_{0},\nu}(w)}\right\|\leq\sup_{|\alpha/\alpha_{0}-1|\leq 1/4}\frac{2(\nu+d/2)}{\alpha}\leq\frac{4(2\nu+d)}{3\alpha_{0}},

which is a constant that depends only on
\alpha_{0},\nu,d
. Thus the double supremum in (
S4
) is upper bounded by a constant for the isotropic Matérn covariance function in Example
1
.

(ii) The tapered isotropic Matérn covariance function (
Kaufman
et al. 2008
) has the following spectral density for any
(\theta,\alpha,\nu)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{3}
:

\displaystyle f_{\theta,\alpha,\nu}(w)=f_{\tap}(w)\cdot\frac{\Gamma(\nu+d/2)}{\Gamma(\nu)\pi^{d/2}}\frac{\theta}{(\alpha^{2}+\|w\|^{2})^{\nu+d/2}},

(S.52)

where
f_{\tap}
satisfies
0<f_{\tap}(w)\leq C_{\tap}(1+\|w\|^{2})^{-(\nu+d/2+\eta_{\tap})}
for some constants
C_{\tap}>0,\eta_{\tap}>0
and all
w\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. The function
f_{\tap}(w)
only depends on
\nu,d
but not
\alpha
and
\theta
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\left|\frac{\frac{\partial f_{\theta_{0},\alpha,\nu}(w)}{\partial\alpha}}{f_{\theta_{0},\alpha_{0},\nu}(w)}\right|=\frac{2(\nu+d/2)\alpha}{\alpha^{2}+\|w\|^{2}},

and hence

\displaystyle\sup_{|\alpha/\alpha_{0}-1|\leq r_{0}}\sup_{w\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\|\frac{\frac{\partial f_{\theta_{0},\alpha,\nu}(w)}{\partial\alpha}}{f_{\theta_{0},\alpha_{0},\nu}(w)}\right\|\leq\sup_{|\alpha/\alpha_{0}-1|\leq 1/4}\frac{2(\nu+d/2)}{\alpha}\leq\frac{4(2\nu+d)}{3\alpha_{0}},

which is a constant that depends only on
\alpha_{0},\nu,d
. Thus the double supremum in (
S4
) is upper bounded by a constant for the tapered isotropic Matérn covariance function in Example
2
.

(iii) The isotropic generalized Wendland covariance function has the following spectral density for any
\mu>\nu+d
,
\nu\geq 1/2
, and
(\theta,\alpha)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}
(Theorem 1 of
Bevilacqua et al. 2019
):

\displaystyle f_{\theta,\alpha,\nu}(w)

\displaystyle=\theta\cdot\frac{\Gamma(\mu+1)\Gamma(2\nu+d-1)}{2^{d+\nu-3/2}\pi^{d/2}\Gamma(\nu+(d-1)/2)\Gamma(\mu+2\nu+d)}\cdot\frac{\Gamma(\nu-1/2)}{2^{3/2-\nu}B(2\nu-1,\mu+1)}

\displaystyle\quad\cdot\alpha^{-(2\nu+d)}{}_{1}F_{2}\left(\nu+\frac{d}{2};\nu+\frac{d+\mu}{2},\nu+\frac{d+\mu+1}{2};-\frac{\|w\|^{2}}{4\alpha^{2}}\right),

(S.53)

where
\Gamma(\nu-1/2)/\big\{2^{3/2-\nu}B(2\nu-1,\mu+1)\big\}
is defined to be 1 if
\nu=1/2
, and for any
a,b,c\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
,

{}_{1}F_{2}(a;b,c;z)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{(a)_{k}z^{k}}{(b)_{k}(c)_{k}k!},\quad\text{for }z\in\mathbb{R},

with
(x)_{k}=\Gamma(x+k)/\Gamma(x)
for any
x>0
being the Pochhammer symbol. The derivative of
{}_{1}F_{2}(a;b,c;z)
is

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}z}{}_{1}F_{2}(a;b,c;z)=\frac{a}{bc}{}_{1}F_{2}(a+1;b+1,c+1;z).

Therefore, we can calculate that

\displaystyle\frac{\frac{\partial f_{\theta_{0},\alpha,\nu}(w)}{\partial\alpha}}{f_{\theta_{0},\alpha_{0},\nu}(w)}

\displaystyle=\frac{-(2\nu+d)\alpha^{-(2\nu+d+1)}{}_{1}F_{2}\left(\nu+\frac{d}{2};\nu+\frac{d+\mu}{2},\nu+\frac{d+\mu+1}{2};-\frac{\|w\|^{2}}{4\alpha^{2}}\right)}{\alpha^{-(2\nu+d)}{}_{1}F_{2}\left(\nu+\frac{d}{2};\nu+\frac{d+\mu}{2},\nu+\frac{d+\mu+1}{2};-\frac{\|w\|^{2}}{4\alpha^{2}}\right)}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{\|w\|^{2}\alpha^{-(2\nu+d+3)}\frac{2\nu+d}{(2\nu+d+\mu)(2\nu+d+\mu+1)}{}_{1}F_{2}\left(\nu+\frac{d}{2}+1;\nu+\frac{d+\mu}{2}+1,\nu+\frac{d+\mu+3}{2};-\frac{\|w\|^{2}}{4\alpha^{2}}\right)}{\alpha^{-(2\nu+d)}{}_{1}F_{2}\left(\nu+\frac{d}{2};\nu+\frac{d+\mu}{2},\nu+\frac{d+\mu+1}{2};-\frac{\|w\|^{2}}{4\alpha^{2}}\right)}.

Part (iii) in Theorems 1 and 2 of
Bevilacqua et al. 2019
have shown that as
\|w\|\to\infty
,
f_{\theta,\alpha,\nu}(w)\asymp\|w\|^{-(2\nu+d)}
for a given set of
\theta,\alpha,\nu,\mu
. Furthermore, the function
{}_{1}F_{2}(a;b,c;z)
is strictly positive and takes finite values for all
z\in[0,+\infty)
. Therefore, there exist
0<c_{1}(\mu,\nu,d,\alpha)<C_{1}(\mu,\nu,d,\alpha)<\infty
and
0<c_{2}(\mu,\nu,d,\alpha)<C_{2}(\mu,\nu,d,\alpha)<\infty
which are all continuous functions in
\alpha
, such that

\displaystyle c_{1}(\mu,\nu,d,\alpha)\|w\|^{-(2\nu+d+2)}\leq{}_{1}F_{2}\left(\nu+\frac{d}{2}+1;\nu+\frac{d+\mu}{2}+1,\nu+\frac{d+\mu+3}{2};-\frac{\|w\|^{2}}{4\alpha^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq C_{1}(\mu,\nu,d,\alpha)\|w\|^{-(2\nu+d+2)},

\displaystyle c_{2}(\mu,\nu,d,\alpha)\|w\|^{-(2\nu+d)}\leq{}_{1}F_{2}\left(\nu+\frac{d}{2};\nu+\frac{d+\mu}{2},\nu+\frac{d+\mu+1}{2};-\frac{\|w\|^{2}}{4\alpha^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq C_{2}(\mu,\nu,d,\alpha)\|w\|^{-(2\nu+d)}.

Therefore, for all
\alpha
that satisfies
|\alpha/\alpha_{0}-1|\leq 1/4
, for all
w\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

\displaystyle\left|\frac{\frac{\partial f_{\theta_{0},\alpha,\nu}(w)}{\partial\alpha}}{f_{\theta_{0},\alpha_{0},\nu}(w)}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{4(2\nu+d)}{3\alpha_{0}}+\frac{64\sup_{|\alpha/\alpha_{0}-1|\leq 1/4}C_{1}(\mu,\nu,d,\alpha)}{27\alpha_{0}^{3}\inf_{|\alpha/\alpha_{0}-1|\leq 1/4}c_{2}(\mu,\nu,d,\alpha)},

which is a finite number that depends on
\mu,\nu,d,\alpha_{0}
. Thus the double supremum in (
S4
) is upper bounded by a constant for the isotropic generalized Wendland covariance function in Example
3
.

(iv) The isotropic confluent hypergeometric covariance function (
Ma and Bhadra 2022
) has the following spectral density for any
(\theta,\alpha,\nu,\mu)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{4}
:

\displaystyle f_{\theta,\alpha,\nu,\mu}(w)

\displaystyle=\frac{\theta\cdot 2^{\nu-\mu}\nu^{\nu}}{\Gamma(\nu+\mu)\pi^{d/2}\alpha^{2(\nu+\mu)}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(2\nu/t+\|w\|^{2}\right)^{-(\nu+d/2)}t^{-(\nu+\mu+1)}\exp\left\{-1/(2\alpha^{2}t)\right\}\mathrm{d}t.

(S.54)

We highlight the dependence on
\mu
in the spectral density for convenience of expressions below, though we have assumed that
\mu
is known. The proof of Proposition 1 in
Ma and Bhadra 2022
has shown that

\displaystyle\lim_{\|w\|\to\infty}\frac{f_{\theta,\alpha,\nu,\mu}(w)}{\frac{2^{2\nu}\nu^{\nu}}{\pi^{d/2}}\theta\|w\|^{-(2\nu+d)}\left(\frac{2\nu\alpha^{2}\|w\|^{2}}{2\nu\alpha^{2}\|w\|^{2}+1}\right)^{\nu+d/2}}=1.

(S.55)

For the derivative with respect to
\alpha
, we can calculate from (
S.54
) that

\displaystyle\left|\frac{\frac{\partial f_{\theta_{0},\alpha,\nu,\mu}(w)}{\partial\alpha}}{f_{\theta_{0},\alpha_{0},\nu,\mu}(w)}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2(\nu+\mu)}{\alpha}+\frac{2(\nu+\mu+1)}{\alpha}\cdot\frac{f_{\theta_{0},\alpha,\nu,\mu+1}(w)}{f_{\theta_{0},\alpha,\nu,\mu}(w)}.

(S.56)

From (
S.54
), we clearly have that
0<f_{\theta,\alpha,\nu,\mu}(w)<\infty
for all
w\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
f_{\theta,\alpha,\nu,\mu}(w)
is always a continuous function in
\|w\|
and
\alpha
. Therefore, from (
S.55
), there exists a function
0<C_{1}(\alpha,\theta_{0},\nu,\mu)<\infty
continuous in
\alpha
, such that
\sup_{w\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}f_{\theta_{0},\alpha,\nu,\mu+1}(w)/f_{\theta_{0},\alpha,\nu,\mu}(w)\leq C_{1}(\alpha,\theta_{0},\nu,\mu)
. Hence, we obtain from (
S.56
) that for all
\alpha
that satisfies
|\alpha/\alpha_{0}-1|\leq 1/4
, for all
w\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

\displaystyle\left|\frac{\frac{\partial f_{\theta_{0},\alpha,\nu,\mu}(w)}{\partial\alpha}}{f_{\theta_{0},\alpha_{0},\nu,\mu}(w)}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{8(\nu+\mu)}{3\alpha_{0}}+\frac{8(\nu+\mu+1)}{3\alpha_{0}}\cdot\sup_{|\alpha/\alpha_{0}-1|\leq 1/4}C_{1}(\alpha,\theta_{0},\nu,\mu),

which is a finite number that depends on
\mu,\nu,d,\alpha_{0}
. Thus the double supremum in (
S4
) is upper bounded by a constant for the isotropic confluent hypergeometric covariance function in Example
4
.

Checking (ii) of Assumption
2
:

From (
S.51
), the spectral density of isotropic Matérn covariance function is clearly a decreasing function in
\alpha
. From (
S.52
), the spectral density of the tapered isotropic Matérn is also decreasing in
\alpha
since
f_{\tap}
does not depend on
\alpha
. The proof of Lemma 1 in
Bevilacqua et al. 2019
has shown that under our condition
\mu>\nu+d
, the spectral density of generalized Wendland in (
S4
) is a decreasing function in
\alpha
. The proof of Lemma 4 in
Ma and Bhadra 2022
has shown that for fixed
\theta,\nu,\mu
, the spectral density of confluent hypergeometric covariance function in (
S.54
) is a decreasing function in
\alpha
. This completes the proof of Proposition
1
.
∎

S5
Proof of Theorem
4

Proof of
Theorem
4
.

We first recall from Theorem
3
that for the higher-order quadratic variation estimators
\widehat{\theta}_{n}
and
\widehat{\tau}_{n}
, they satisfy Assumption
4
with
b_{1}
and
b_{2}
defined by

\displaystyle b_{1}

\displaystyle=\min\Bigg\{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2(1-\gamma)\nu}{d}-\rho_{22},~\frac{1-\gamma}{2}-\varsigma,~\frac{1}{4}+\frac{(1-\gamma)(\ell_{\star}-2\nu)}{d}-\frac{\rho_{1}+\rho_{22}}{2},

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\frac{1-\gamma}{4}+\frac{(1-\gamma)(\ell_{\star}-\nu)}{d}-\frac{\rho_{1}}{2}-\varsigma\Bigg\},

\displaystyle b_{2}

\displaystyle=\min\Bigg\{\frac{1}{2},~\frac{(1-\gamma)(4\nu+d)}{2d}-\rho_{21}-\varsigma,~\frac{1}{4}+\frac{(1-\gamma)\ell_{\star}}{d}-\frac{\rho_{1}+\rho_{21}}{2},~

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\frac{(1-\gamma)(4\nu+d+4\ell_{\star})}{4d}-\frac{\rho_{1}+2\rho_{21}}{2}-\varsigma\Bigg\}.

(S.57)

And recall that
\gamma
needs to satisfy
\max\big\{1-d/(4\nu),0\big\}<\gamma<1
.

In the expression of
b_{1}
above, we first consider the third and fourth terms inside the minimum. Given that
\ell_{\star}=\lceil\nu+d/2\rceil\geq\nu+d/2
, the fourth term satisfies

\displaystyle\frac{1-\gamma}{4}+\frac{(1-\gamma)(\ell_{\star}-\nu)}{d}-\frac{\rho_{1}}{2}-\varsigma\geq\frac{1-\gamma}{4}+\frac{1-\gamma}{2}-\frac{\rho_{1}}{2}-\varsigma.

Therefore, when
\rho_{1}
and
\varsigma
are sufficiently small, the fourth term is lower bounded by the second term inside the minimum in the expression of
b_{1}
.

For the third term inside the minimum in the expression of
b_{1}
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}+\frac{(1-\gamma)(\ell_{\star}-2\nu)}{d}-\frac{\rho_{1}+\rho_{22}}{2}\geq\frac{1}{4}+\frac{(1-\gamma)(\nu+d/2-2\nu)}{d}-\frac{\rho_{1}+\rho_{22}}{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left[\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2(1-\gamma)\nu}{d}\right]+\frac{1-\gamma}{2}-\frac{\rho_{1}+\rho_{22}}{2}.

In other words, when
\rho_{1},\rho_{22},\varsigma
are sufficiently small, the third term is approximately a linear combination of the first and second terms inside the minimum in the expression of
b_{1}
. As a result, it is sufficient to balance the first and second terms inside the minimum in the expression of
b_{1}
in (
S5
). We set them equal and obtain that
\gamma=4\nu/(4\nu+d)
. Then we have from (
S5
) that when
\gamma=4\nu/(4\nu+d)
,

\displaystyle b_{1}

\displaystyle\geq\min\Bigg\{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2(1-\gamma)\nu}{d}-\rho_{22},~\frac{1-\gamma}{2}-\varsigma,~\frac{1}{2}\left[\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2(1-\gamma)\nu}{d}\right]+\frac{1-\gamma}{2}-\frac{\rho_{1}+\rho_{22}}{2},

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\frac{3(1-\gamma)}{4}-\frac{\rho_{1}}{2}-\varsigma\Bigg\}

\displaystyle=\min\Bigg\{\frac{d}{2(4\nu+d)}-\rho_{22},~~\frac{d}{2(4\nu+d)}-\varsigma,~~\frac{3d}{4(4\nu+d)}-\frac{\rho_{1}+\rho_{22}}{2},

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\frac{3d}{4(4\nu+d)}-\frac{\rho_{1}}{2}-\varsigma\Bigg\},

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2(4\nu/d+1)}-\varrho,

(S.58)

where
\varrho=\max(\rho_{22},\rho_{1}/2)
since
\varsigma>0
can be arbitrarily small. As such, the condition in (
3.1
) becomes

\displaystyle 0<\rho_{1}<\frac{d}{4\nu+d},\quad 0<\rho_{21}<\frac{2\nu}{4\nu+d},\quad 0<\rho_{22}<\frac{d}{2(4\nu+d)},\quad\rho_{31}>0,\quad 0<\rho_{32}<\frac{1}{4\nu+d},

which are satisfied by (
4
) of Theorem
4
.

In the expression of
b_{2}
above, since
0<1-\gamma<\min\{1,d/(4\nu)\}
, we can choose
1-\gamma
sufficiently close to its upper bound, say
1-\gamma=\min\{1,d/(4\nu)\}-\eta
for some small
\eta\in(0,\min\{1,d/(4\nu)\})
, such that

\displaystyle\quad~\frac{(1-\gamma)(4\nu+d)}{2d}-\rho_{21}-\varsigma

\displaystyle=\frac{\min\{1,d/(4\nu)\}(4\nu+d)}{2d}-\frac{\eta(4\nu+d)}{2d}-\rho_{21}-\varsigma

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}+\min\left\{\frac{2\nu}{d},\frac{d}{8\nu}\right\}-\frac{\eta(4\nu+d)}{2d}-\rho_{21}-\varsigma,

(S.59)

\displaystyle\quad~\frac{1}{4}+\frac{(1-\gamma)\ell_{\star}}{d}-\frac{\rho_{1}+\rho_{21}}{2}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{4}+\frac{(\nu+d/2)\min\{1,d/(4\nu)\}}{d}-\frac{\eta(\nu+d/2)}{d}-\frac{\rho_{1}+\rho_{21}}{2}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}+\min\left\{\frac{1}{4}+\frac{\nu}{d},\frac{d}{8\nu}\right\}-\frac{\eta(\nu+d/2)}{d}-\frac{\rho_{1}+\rho_{21}}{2},

(S.60)

\displaystyle\quad~\frac{(1-\gamma)(4\nu+d+4\ell_{\star})}{4d}-\frac{\rho_{1}+2\rho_{21}}{2}-\varsigma

\displaystyle\geq\frac{(8\nu+3d)\min\{1,d/(4\nu)\}}{4d}-\frac{\eta(8\nu+3d)}{4d}-\frac{\rho_{1}+2\rho_{21}}{2}-\varsigma

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}+\min\left\{\frac{1}{4}+\frac{2\nu}{d},\frac{3d}{16\nu}\right\}-\frac{\eta(8\nu+3d)}{4d}-\frac{\rho_{1}+2\rho_{21}}{2}-\varsigma.

(S.61)

In order to make the right-hand sides of (
S.59
), (
S.60
), and (
S.61
) strictly larger than
1/2
, it is sufficient to have

\displaystyle\rho_{21}<\min\left\{\frac{2\nu}{d},\frac{d}{8\nu}\right\},~~\frac{\rho_{1}+\rho_{21}}{2}<\min\left\{\frac{1}{4}+\frac{\nu}{d},\frac{d}{8\nu}\right\},

\displaystyle\frac{\rho_{1}+2\rho_{21}}{2}<\min\left\{\frac{1}{4}+\frac{2\nu}{d},\frac{3d}{16\nu}\right\},

(S.62)

since both
\eta>0
and
\varsigma>0
can be chosen as arbitrarily small. We notice that the third relations in (
S5
) is implied by adding up the first two relations in (
S5
), which are included in (
4
) of Theorem
4
. Therefore, with such choice of
\gamma
, we can set
b_{2}=1/2
in (
S5
). Finally, the conclusion of Theorem
4
follows by combining the lower bound of
b_{1}
in (
S5
) and
b_{2}=1/2
together with the posterior convergence in Theorem
2
.
∎

S6
Proof of Proposition
2

Proof of
Proposition
2
.

First, we verify Assumption
3
. Clearly the joint prior density
\pi(\theta,\alpha,\tau,\beta)
is continuous everywhere and satisfies
\pi(\theta_{0},\alpha_{0},\tau_{0},\beta_{0})>0
. Furthermore, since
\tau\sim\textup{IG}(a_{2},b_{2})
, we have that for any
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\tau^{-n/2}\pi(\tau)\mathrm{d}\tau

\displaystyle=\frac{b_{2}^{a_{2}}}{\Gamma(a_{2})}\int_{0}^{\infty}\tau^{-n/2}\cdot\tau^{-(a_{2}+1)}\exp(-b_{2}/\tau)\mathrm{d}\tau=\frac{\Gamma(a_{2}+n/2)}{b_{2}^{n/2}\Gamma(a_{2})}<\infty.

Therefore Assumption
3
is satisfied.

Next, we verify Assumption
5
. For the isotropic Matérn covariance function, Assumption
2
is satisfied with
\kappa=1
. We first decompose the set
\Ecal_{n}^{c}
based on Equation
16
into several sets:

\displaystyle\Ecal_{n}^{c}

\displaystyle\subseteq\{\|\beta\|^{2}/\theta>n^{\rho_{1}}\}\cup\{\tau/\theta<n^{-\rho_{21}}\}\cup\{\tau/\theta>n^{\rho_{22}}\}\cup\{\alpha<n^{-\rho_{31}}\}\cup\{\alpha>n^{\rho_{32}}\}.

(S.63)

We show each of the set on the right-hand side of (
S.63
) satisfies Assumption
5
with the prior specified in Proposition
2
. Recall that for the isotropic Matérn in Example
1
, we have
\kappa=1
from the proof of Proposition
1
.

First, given
\beta\sim\Ncal(0,a_{0}I_{p})
,
\theta\sim\text{IG}(a_{1},b_{1})
, we have that
\|\beta\|^{2}/a_{0}\sim\chi^{2}_{p}
, and hence for sufficiently large
n
,

\displaystyle\Pi(\|\beta\|^{2}/\theta>n^{\rho_{1}})

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{b_{1}^{a_{1}}}{\Gamma(a_{1})}\theta^{-(a_{1}+1)}\mathrm{e}^{-b_{1}/\theta}\int_{n^{\rho_{1}}\theta/a_{0}}^{\infty}\frac{1}{2^{p}\Gamma(p/2)}z^{p/2-1}\mathrm{e}^{-z/2}\mathrm{d}z\mathrm{d}\theta

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{n^{-\rho_{1}/2}}\frac{b_{1}^{a_{1}}}{\Gamma(a_{1})}\theta^{-(a_{1}+1)}\mathrm{e}^{-b_{1}/\theta}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{2^{p}\Gamma(p/2)}z^{p/2-1}\mathrm{e}^{-z/2}\mathrm{d}z\mathrm{d}\theta

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{n^{-\rho_{1}/2}}^{\infty}\frac{b_{1}^{a_{1}}}{\Gamma(a_{1})}\theta^{-(a_{1}+1)}\mathrm{e}^{-b_{1}/\theta}\int_{n^{\rho_{1}/2}/a_{0}}^{\infty}\frac{1}{2^{p}\Gamma(p/2)}z^{p/2-1}\mathrm{e}^{-z/2}\mathrm{d}z\mathrm{d}\theta

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(i)}}{{\leq}}\int_{n^{\rho_{1}/2}}^{\infty}\frac{b_{1}^{a_{1}}}{\Gamma(a_{1})}t^{a_{1}-1}\mathrm{e}^{-b_{1}t}\mathrm{d}t+\int_{n^{\rho_{1}/2}/a_{0}}^{\infty}\frac{1}{2^{p}\Gamma(p/2)}z^{p/2-1}\mathrm{e}^{-z/2}\mathrm{d}z

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(ii)}}{{<}}\int_{n^{\rho_{1}/2}}^{\infty}\frac{b_{1}^{a_{1}}}{\Gamma(a_{1})}\mathrm{e}^{-b_{1}t/2}\mathrm{d}t+\int_{n^{\rho_{1}/2}/a_{0}}^{\infty}\frac{1}{2^{p}\Gamma(p/2)}\mathrm{e}^{-z/4}\mathrm{d}z

\displaystyle=\frac{2b_{1}^{a_{1}-1}}{\Gamma(a_{1})}\exp\big(-b_{1}n^{\rho_{1}/2}/2\big)+\frac{1}{2^{p-2}\Gamma(p/2)}\exp\big\{-n^{\rho_{1}/2}/(4a_{0})\big\}

\displaystyle\prec n^{-(3p+6)},

(S.64)

where for (i) we use a change of variable
t=1/\theta
, and (ii) follows when
n
is sufficiently large such that
z^{p/2-1}<\mathrm{e}^{z/4}
for all
z\geq n^{\rho_{1}/2}/a_{0}
.

Second, given
\tau\sim\text{IG}(a_{2},b_{2})
,
\theta\sim\text{IG}(a_{1},b_{1})
, we have that

\displaystyle\Pi(\tau/\theta<n^{-\rho_{21}})

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{b_{1}^{a_{1}}}{\Gamma(a_{1})}\theta^{-(a_{1}+1)}\mathrm{e}^{-b_{1}/\theta}\int_{0}^{n^{-\rho_{21}}\theta}\frac{b_{2}^{a_{2}}}{\Gamma(a_{2})}\tau^{-(a_{2}+1)}\mathrm{e}^{-b_{2}/\tau}\mathrm{d}\tau\mathrm{d}\theta

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{n^{\rho_{21}/2}}\frac{b_{1}^{a_{1}}}{\Gamma(a_{1})}\theta^{-(a_{1}+1)}\mathrm{e}^{-b_{1}/\theta}\int_{0}^{n^{-\rho_{21}/2}}\frac{b_{2}^{a_{2}}}{\Gamma(a_{2})}\tau^{-(a_{2}+1)}\mathrm{e}^{-b_{2}/\tau}\mathrm{d}\tau\mathrm{d}\theta

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{n^{\rho_{21}/2}}^{\infty}\frac{b_{1}^{a_{1}}}{\Gamma(a_{1})}\theta^{-(a_{1}+1)}\mathrm{e}^{-b_{1}/\theta}\int_{0}^{n^{-\rho_{21}}\theta}\frac{b_{2}^{a_{2}}}{\Gamma(a_{2})}\tau^{-(a_{2}+1)}\mathrm{e}^{-b_{2}/\tau}\mathrm{d}\tau\mathrm{d}\theta

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{n^{-\rho_{21}/2}}\frac{b_{2}^{a_{2}}}{\Gamma(a_{2})}\tau^{-(a_{2}+1)}\mathrm{e}^{-b_{2}/\tau}\mathrm{d}\tau+\int_{n^{\rho_{21}/2}}^{\infty}\frac{b_{1}^{a_{1}}}{\Gamma(a_{1})}\theta^{-(a_{1}+1)}\mathrm{e}^{-b_{1}/\theta}\mathrm{d}\theta

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(i)}}{{=}}\int_{n^{\rho_{21}/2}}^{\infty}\frac{b_{2}^{a_{2}}}{\Gamma(a_{2})}t^{a_{2}-1}\mathrm{e}^{-b_{2}t}\mathrm{d}t+\int_{n^{\rho_{21}/2}}^{\infty}\frac{b_{1}^{a_{1}}}{\Gamma(a_{1})}\theta^{-(a_{1}+1)}\mathrm{e}^{-b_{1}/\theta}\mathrm{d}\theta

\displaystyle\leq\int_{n^{\rho_{21}/2}}^{\infty}\frac{b_{2}^{a_{2}}}{\Gamma(a_{2})}\mathrm{e}^{-b_{2}t/2}\mathrm{d}t+\int_{n^{\rho_{21}/2}}^{\infty}\frac{b_{1}^{a_{1}}}{\Gamma(a_{1})}\theta^{-(a_{1}+1)}\mathrm{d}\theta

\displaystyle=\frac{2b_{2}^{a_{2}-1}}{\Gamma(a_{2})}\exp\big(-b_{2}n^{\rho_{21}/2}/2\big)+\frac{b_{1}^{a_{1}}}{a_{1}\Gamma(a_{1})}n^{-a_{1}\rho_{21}/2}

\displaystyle\prec n^{-(3p+6)},

(S.65)

if
a_{1}\rho_{21}/2>3p+6
, or
a_{1}>2(3p+6)/\rho_{21}
, where in (i) we use the change of variable
t=1/\tau
.

Third, for the right tail of
\tau/\theta
, we have that

\displaystyle\Pi(\tau/\theta>n^{\rho_{22}})
